# 2026-09-12 — R18 — ai-assisted-review-workflows (bundle, Deep) — decision (revision 6)

## Decision

**Status.** This decision is a **proposal to be validated in the implementation phase against the real template**. `rs-launch-blueprint` has no `.github/workflows/` tree, no Rust code and no configured credential today; P02 is research and P03 builds the template, and P03's CI is what proves or disproves what is recorded here. A recommendation that does not survive implementation is **revised against this recorded research**, not researched again from scratch.

**Revision 6 reverses part of a published decision, which is why this entry carries a `## Supersedes` section** — the only H2 beyond the four that the approved spec permits, and permitted for exactly this case. Revision 5 was published and carried two approving audits. On **2026-09-12 the owner gave direction that amends it**, and revision 6 implements that direction rather than re-researching the item. The direction has five parts: both workflow files ship with the template by default and there is **no enable variable**, so `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_REVIEW` and `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT` are removed entirely; a lane **runs when the credential it needs is present** and, when the credential is absent, does not run, does not error, and prints which key needs setting so a reader can see it and act; anyone who does not want a lane **deletes the file**; **both lanes must reject bots**; and the author-trust test **stays the `author_association` check already specified**, because the owner considered a genuine write-permission check and chose association as the more accurate test. Revisions 1 to 5 said "nothing below claims owner acceptance: the owner has reviewed R01 only". That sentence is withdrawn as of this date: owner direction on this item now exists, it is recorded above, and every place where revision 5 made its recommendation conditional on an acceptance the owner had not given is rewritten rather than merely annotated. What the direction does **not** change is the residual analysis, which this revision restates exactly as strongly as revision 5 did.

**Recommendation — restated at revision 6, unconditionally.** Ship **both** workflow files, as **one bundle**, from `anthropics/claude-code-action` at **`v1.0.220`** (annotated tag `020e931323e467e00b803ad64e03410d3796b8f7` → commit `19dda84776b3518d98b8798e591daee763049ed3`, resolved 2026-09-10), **with the template, by default, with no enable variable**. Each lane is gated on **credential presence**: a lane runs when the credential it needs is configured, and when it is not the lane is skipped with a printed reason rather than failing. A repository that does not want a lane **deletes its file**. **The conditional form revision 4 introduced and revision 5 carried is dissolved, not withheld.** Revision 4 recommended shipping both files as inert artifacts and withheld enablement on any public repository until the owner recorded acceptance of the residual named under "What installing a credential costs the owner" below. Under the owner's 2026-09-12 design there is no flag for the owner to set, so there is no separate act of enabling left to accept: **installing a credential is itself the act of acceptance, and it is taken by whoever installs it** — the origin-repository owner for the origin repository, each adopter for their own clone. **One case that sentence does not cover**, and it is stated in full under "What installing a credential costs the owner" below rather than softened: an adopter whose repository already holds `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` or `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` for an unrelated purpose takes no act of acceptance at all, sees nothing printed, and gets both lanes live on the next qualifying event. That is the whole of what changed about the recommendation's form. **The residual did not change and is stated exactly as strongly as before**: it is unremovable by configuration, it binds both lanes, and it is spelled out under "What installing a credential costs the owner" and written into both fixture headers. Carried unchanged from revision 4: the pin, the `pull-requests: write` grant on the review job, the removal of the run-time plugin-marketplace fetch, the `author_association` prefilter on the assistant lane, and the `draft == false` clause on the review lane. **New at revision 6, beyond removing the two variables**: the credential-presence preflight described below, **bot exclusion on the assistant lane**, which revision 5 did not have, and the handling of **both credential shapes** rather than federation alone. That an unconfigured clone yields a `skipped` job rather than a red check is no longer carried as an open unknown: on owner instruction it is an **acceptance criterion the implementation phase must prove** (B4, and U2 restated as such).

**The mechanism, verified rather than assumed.** The owner chose, on 2026-09-11, an always-run preflight job that prints the reason for skipping and gates the real job behind it. That is the mechanism revision 6 writes, and the three facts it rests on were each checked again for this revision rather than carried from the earlier text:

1. **A job-level `if:` cannot read `secrets`.** The platform's context table lists `github`, `needs`, `vars` and `inputs` for `jobs.<job_id>.if` (`contexts-availability.log`), and `actionlint` rejects `if: secrets.CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN != ''` with exit 1 and the diagnostic `context "secrets" is not allowed here. available contexts are "github", "inputs", "needs", "vars"`. That control was **re-run for revision 6**, not cited: `actionlint.log`, revision-6 addendum, block R6-2.
2. **`secrets` *is* available in `jobs.<job_id>.env`.** Both revision-6 fixtures put four credential reads in a job-level `env` block and lint clean (`actionlint.log`, R6-1 and R6-3). That is what makes the relay possible at all.
3. **`needs` *is* available in a job-level `if:`, and a skipped dependency skips its dependents.** GitHub's workflow-syntax reference for `jobs.<job_id>.needs` says: "If a job fails or is skipped, all jobs that need it are skipped unless the jobs use a conditional expression that causes the job to continue" (`needs-and-credential-shapes.log`, block N1). Neither real job uses such an expression, so a skipped preflight skips it. **That leg is documentation, not an observed run**, and it is why B4 is an acceptance criterion rather than a settled claim.

**The gate is written as a positive predicate, and that choice is a correctness fix rather than a style preference.** A skipped job's outputs are **empty**, so if the preflight is skipped — which is exactly what a rejected fork, draft, bot or `author_association` guard does to it — then `needs.credential-preflight.outputs.credential` is the empty string and a negative gate, `!= 'none'`, evaluates **true**. Written that way, the real job's skip would rest *entirely* on the skip-propagation rule quoted above, which is the one leg of the mechanism this research has not observed. The fixtures therefore gate on `== 'static' || == 'federation'`, which is false on an empty output as well as on `'none'`, so the guards bind the real job **through the value as well as through the dependency**. The decision relies on skip propagation anyway, for the case of a guard rejecting the event; it does not rely on it *alone* for the case that matters most, a bot-authored comment on a credentialed public repository. Both fixtures carried the negative form when the first revision-6 lint addendum was written and were corrected afterwards; the correction, its reason and its own clean lint are the second addendum in `actionlint.log` (blocks R6-5 to R6-7), and `grep -c "outputs.credential != "` now returns **0** for both fixtures. **This is why B4 and B10 assert the *real* job's conclusion rather than the preflight's**: asserting only that the preflight was skipped would pass while the real job ran.

So the preflight job **reads credential presence into an environment value, prints the skip reason, and publishes a literal output that both real jobs gate on through `needs`**. Its output is one of the three strings `static`, `federation` or `none` — never a secret-derived value, which would be masked. It carries `permissions: {}`, no checkout, and always exits 0. **On the `shape = none` branch, and only there**, it prints through both channels a reader actually sees: a `::notice title=…::` annotation on the run, and a `$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY` block naming the exact secret or variable names to set and saying that deleting the file is the supported way to opt out. On every other branch it prints one line of ordinary log text and nothing else, which is why a repository whose credential predates these files is told nothing — see the paragraph after "Given (1) to (4)". **The preflight is "always-run" only in the sense that it is not gated on credential presence, because it cannot be.** It still carries every trigger, fork, draft, bot and author-trust guard, because a preflight with no guards would spin a runner for every qualifying event and would falsify this decision's own claim that an event the guards reject spends no runner minutes — the claim `#1068`'s first exposure and **U12** are about. The cost of the design, stated rather than minimised: one short `ubuntu-latest` job per event that passes the guards, even when no credential is configured.

**Both credential shapes are handled, because the owner has not said which they have.** Federation uses repository **variables**, which a job condition can read directly; an API key or an OAuth token is a **secret**, which it cannot. The preflight detects either, and the shapes, their precedence and their costs are read in source at `v1.0.220` (`needs-and-credential-shapes.log`, block N2):

| Shape | What the adopter sets | How the preflight detects it | What it costs to set up |
|---|---|---|---|
| Static credential | repository secret `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, or `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` | job-level `env:` read of `secrets.*`, non-empty | one Anthropic Console API key, or one `claude setup-token` run for a Pro/Max subscription. **No Anthropic organization admin rights.** |
| Workload identity federation | repository variables `ANTHROPIC_FEDERATION_RULE_ID` and `ANTHROPIC_ORGANIZATION_ID`; `ANTHROPIC_SERVICE_ACCOUNT_ID` optional | job-level `env:` read of `vars.*`, both non-empty — mirroring the action's own predicate | **admin access to an Anthropic organization**: register an issuer, create a service account, create a federation rule (`docs/setup.md`). No stored credential to rotate. Behaviour change: `classify_inline_comments` is skipped, so unconfirmed inline comments are posted directly |

**The precedence, in the words of the action's own setup documentation**: "Do not set `anthropic_api_key` or `claude_code_oauth_token` alongside the federation inputs — **a static credential takes precedence and federation will not be used**" (`docs/setup.md` at `v1.0.220`, quoted in the F051+F052 adjudication below). Revision 6 confirms that in source rather than resting on the prose: `setupWorkloadIdentity()` at `base-action/src/workload-identity.ts:128-135` returns `undefined` and emits `core.warning("Workload identity federation inputs are set alongside anthropic_api_key or claude_code_oauth_token. The API key/OAuth token takes precedence, so federation will not be used.")`. **An adopter therefore sets exactly one shape.** Two further source facts make the single action step legal: federation is detected on **two** identifiers, not three — `isWorkloadIdentityConfigured()` at `:44-48` requires `ANTHROPIC_FEDERATION_RULE_ID && ANTHROPIC_ORGANIZATION_ID`, and `action.yml:79-81` marks the service-account identifier optional — and **an empty value is indistinguishable from an absent one**, because every credential read is `process.env.X?.trim()` and `action.yml:335-336` maps an empty input through `||` to an empty environment value, with **no `default:` key on any of the five credential inputs** (`grep -c 'default:'` over `action.yml:67-82` prints `0`, exit 1, which for `grep -c` *is* the zero-match result). That is why both fixtures can pass all five inputs at once and let the action's own precedence apply. **Which shape this owner has is not known to this research**, and nothing here chooses for them; the earlier preference for federation survives as a preference, recorded under U7, not as a requirement.

**Bot exclusion on the assistant lane — closing a gap, not restating a control.** Revision 5's review-lane fixture carried two bot checks (`github.event.pull_request.user.type != 'Bot'` and `github.event.sender.type != 'Bot'`); its assistant-lane fixture carried **none**. Guard-contract clause (b) below was therefore true of one lane only, and the decision did not say so. The gap is not covered downstream by the action: this decision's own source read found `src/github/validation/permissions.ts:114` is `if (actor.endsWith("[bot]")) { … return true; }`, so `checkWritePermissions` **returns `true` unconditionally for any actor whose login ends in `[bot]`**, and the refusal of App actors lives entirely in `checkHumanActor`, a different function called later and after the App token is minted. Revision 6 adds two **type-based** checks to the assistant lane, per event: `github.event.sender.type != 'Bot'` for the run's initiator, and the triggering content's own author (`github.event.comment.user.type`, `github.event.review.user.type`, `github.event.issue.user.type`) — which differs from the initiator on `issues: [assigned]`. Type-based rather than a name denylist, for the reason `## Engines` §2 already gives: a type check is name-independent and catches every bot without enumeration. Both forms lint clean (`actionlint.log`, R6-1). Their runtime effect is unproven here and is **B10** below.

**The author-trust test is unchanged, and that is a decision, not an omission.** The owner explicitly considered a genuine write-permission check on 2026-09-12 and chose `author_association` as the more accurate test. No write-permission check is added. What `author_association` does not do is still stated with it, unchanged and in the same three places: it is not a write-access check, it does not stop a trusted author who pastes untrusted text, and it would not have stopped the published chain's Phase 2.

Each file is two jobs: a `credential-preflight` job carrying the guards, and the real job gated on the preflight's output through `needs`. The "Job-level guard" column names both.

| File | Trigger | Job-level guard | Permissions | Action inputs |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `.github/workflows/claude-code-review.yml` | `pull_request: types: [opened, ready_for_review, reopened]` | **preflight `if:`** — `py`'s fork guard **and** both `py` bot guards, unchanged **and** `github.event.pull_request.draft == false` (revision 3). **`claude-review` `if:`** — `needs.credential-preflight.outputs.credential == 'static' || … == 'federation'`. **`vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_REVIEW` is removed at revision 6** | `contents: read`, `pull-requests: write`, `issues: read`, `id-token: write` on `claude-review`; `permissions: {}` on the preflight | all five credential inputs, so either shape works: `anthropic_api_key` and `claude_code_oauth_token` from `secrets.*`, three `anthropic_federation_*` identifiers from `vars.*`; `prompt: '/review-pr REPO: … PR_NUMBER: …'`, resolving from a **template-local** `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` that P03 must author. **`plugin_marketplaces` and `plugins` are removed at revision 4** — revision 3 passed `plugin_marketplaces: 'https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code.git'` and `plugins: 'code-review@claude-code-plugins'`, which made this lane's instructions unpinnable |
| `.github/workflows/claude.yml` | `issue_comment: [created]`, `pull_request_review_comment: [created]`, `issues: [opened, assigned]`, `pull_request_review: [submitted]` | **preflight `if:`** — `py`'s four-clause `contains(…, '@claude')` condition **and**, on each of those four clauses, `contains(fromJSON('["OWNER","MEMBER","COLLABORATOR"]'), github.event.<comment\|review\|issue>.author_association)` (revision 3) **and**, **new at revision 6**, `github.event.sender.type != 'Bot'` plus the per-event author type check. **`claude` `if:`** — `needs.credential-preflight.outputs.credential == 'static' || … == 'federation'`. **`vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT` is removed at revision 6** | `contents: write`, `pull-requests: write`, `issues: write`, `id-token: write`, `actions: read` on `claude`; `permissions: {}` on the preflight | the same five credential inputs; `additional_permissions: actions: read`; **no** `prompt:` input (omitting it selects interactive mode — `docs/faq.md` at `v1.0.220`: "**Without `prompt` input**: Runs in interactive mode - waits for @claude mentions in comments", `action-docs-quotes-v1.0.220.log`) |

**Why the assistant lane gains an author-trust prefilter, stated here because it is the security case.** Credential presence protects a **downstream clone**, where no credential is set. It cannot protect a **configured repository**, where the credential is present by definition, and strangers can comment on a public template. **Restated at revision 6, and the substance is unchanged**: revisions 3 to 5 made this argument about `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT`, which was `true` by construction on the origin repository — revisions 3 to 5 and not earlier, because the paragraph itself is new at revision 3, which check 21 confirms; with the variable removed, the thing that is true by construction on a configured repository is the credential. The gap the prefilter fills is the same gap. Upstream issue `anthropics/claude-code-action#1068` — "Default workflow template missing author trust gate — secrets exposed on public repos", open since 2026-03-18, labels `bug`, `documentation`, `area:permissions`, `p1`, author `NONE`, 0 comments, `updated_at` 44 seconds after creation (`issue-1068.log`) — names three exposures of the unprefiltered `if:` and asks for exactly this clause. Revision 2 recorded that issue only as a comment count. Read in full, its three exposures divide as follows against the recommended configuration:

| `#1068`'s exposure | Answered by the credential shape? | Answered by credential presence on a configured repository? | Answered by the revision-3 prefilter? |
|---|---|---|---|
| The job starts and consumes runner minutes | No | No | **Yes**, for any commenter outside `OWNER`/`MEMBER`/`COLLABORATOR` — **U12**, not observed. The prefilter sits on the **preflight** job at revision 6, which is what keeps this row true; a preflight without the guards would make it false |
| `secrets.ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` / `secrets.CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` reach the runner | **Shape-dependent, and rewritten at revision 6.** Under **federation** there is no such secret, so the exposure does not exist. Under a **static credential** the secret does reach the runner for any event that passes the guards, which is `#1068`'s exposure as written. Revisions 3 to 5 recorded only the federation answer, because federation was the only shape they recommended | No | Partly — the prefilter bounds *who* can cause the real job to start, and the preflight itself reads the secret into an `env` value in a job with `permissions: {}` and no checkout. It does not remove the exposure under the static shape |
| An OIDC token is obtainable (`id-token: write`) | **No** | No | **Yes**, same qualification. Note the preflight job requests no `id-token`, so this exposure begins at the real job |

The third row is the correction that matters, and it revises a claim `opus` made and revision 2 carried: federation was recorded as a mitigation for `#1068`, and it answers only the second row. `ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN_REQUEST_TOKEN` and `ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN_REQUEST_URL` are present in the runner environment from the moment a job with `id-token: write` starts, and the 2026-06-01 GMO Flatt Security disclosure describes them as "the credentials required to request that OIDC token, so an attacker who obtains them can replicate the entire token exchange process" — the exchange whose output is a Claude GitHub App installation token with write access to contents, issues, pull requests and workflows (`flatt-disclosure-2026-06-01.log`). Federation changes which credential is worth stealing; it does not remove the one the published chain actually steals. **Scoped at revision 4, because revision 3 overstated it (empirical finding F3), and rewritten at revision 6 because the design changed under it.** Revision 3 said the prefilter "is the only control in this record that acts before the job starts". That was wrong as written, because the assistant lane's job-level `if:` also carried `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT == 'true'`, and a `vars` clause is pre-job control too; revisions 4 and 5 therefore scoped the claim to actor trust once the variable was `true`. **Revision 6 removes the variable, so the scope is restated against what the design now has:** the assistant lane's guards are the `@claude` content match, the `author_association` clause and — new at revision 6 — the two bot type checks, all of them on the preflight job, plus `needs.credential-preflight.outputs.credential == 'static' || … == 'federation'` on the real job. Of those, the **only actor-trust controls** are the `author_association` clause and the bot checks; the `needs` clause is a capability switch, not an actor check, and on a configured repository it is satisfied by construction. The supported claim is therefore: **on a repository with a credential installed, the `author_association` clause and the two bot checks are the only actor-trust controls that act before the job is scheduled.** That is why the prefilter is adopted rather than offered, and it is why the bot checks belong on the preflight rather than only downstream in the action.

**And it is load-bearing for a reason revision 3 did not have, found at revision 4 while correcting F1.** `src/entrypoints/prepare.ts:37` calls `setupGitHubToken()` **before** the write-permission check at `:44` and before either mode calls `checkHumanActor`. `setupGitHubToken()` exchanges the workflow's OIDC token for a Claude GitHub App installation token at `https://api.anthropic.com/api/github/github-app-token-exchange` (`src/github/token.ts:121`). Because the assistant lane passes `additional_permissions`, `parseAdditionalPermissions()` returns a value, so `token.ts:100` sends `DEFAULT_PERMISSIONS` merged with it — `contents: "write"`, `pull_requests: "write"`, `issues: "write"` (`token.ts:69-73`) plus `actions: read`. **A write-capable App installation token is therefore requested before any actor-trust check inside the action returns** (`action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`, E2 and E3). Whether anything attacker-controlled is processed in that window was not traced; that is **U14**. On the review lane `additional_permissions` is unset, `parseAdditionalPermissions()` returns `undefined`, no body is sent, and the resulting permission set is chosen server-side and is **not readable from this source** — recorded as **U17**.

**Ranked runner-up, and revision 6 returns it to being an alternative rather than a pending default.** Ship **neither** workflow and publish a documented opt-in recipe instead (the `codex` and `doxa-openai` position). Revision 4 promoted it from "ranked second" to "the default if the owner withholds acceptance", because at that point the recommendation turned on an acceptance nobody had given. **That promotion is undone at revision 6, and the reason is the design rather than a change of mind**: with no enable variable, the owner's chosen opt-out is *deleting the file*, which every adopter can take unilaterally at any time and which needs no recipe to be published alongside it. The runner-up still wins on any of three owner judgements — it is what an owner would pick who wanted **no** AI workflow file in the template tree at all — but it is no longer the standing outcome of an unanswered question, because the question was answered on 2026-09-12. Two of its three conditions are the ones revision 2 recorded: the owner declines recurring third-party spend on the origin repository, or declines shipping a vendor-AI exemplar inside a public template tree. **The third was added at revision 3, is the one the judgment audit asked to see argued, and is unchanged in force at revision 6**: the owner declines the residual prompt-injection exposure of a **write-capable assistant lane on a public repository, reachable from text an OWNER, MEMBER or COLLABORATOR writes** — because a trusted author routinely quotes untrusted content (pasting an issue body, a fork pull request's diff, a stack trace) into a comment, and neither the prefilter nor any action-side check distinguishes a trusted author's own words from untrusted text that author pasted. The researcher who published the chain states the general form of that residual plainly: "The permission model around Claude Code isn't hardened enough to safely handle untrusted input… at the time of writing, I have reported around 50 separate vulnerabilities to Anthropic that allow attackers to bypass the permission system and execute arbitrary commands in Claude Code" (`flatt-disclosure-2026-06-01.log`). That residual is **not** the published chain — see the exposure analysis under fitness gate 3, which finds the recommended configuration outside every phase of it — but it is a live class, and it is an owner risk decision, not an engineering-fitness one.

The runner-up's *other* stated grounds — that a public template cannot supply a downstream consumer's credential, and that an unconfigured clone would get a red check — are what the credential-presence design is **built** to answer, and the evidence gathered here supports that design **syntactically and by documentation, not yet at runtime**. Precisely what the checks establish (see `## Empirical check`, `contexts-availability.log`, `actionlint.log` and `needs-and-credential-shapes.log`): a job-level `if:` **may** reference the `github`, `needs`, `vars` and `inputs` contexts and **may not** reference `secrets`; `secrets` **is** available in `jobs.<job_id>.env`; and both lanes' guard expressions, including the preflight relay and the new bot checks, lint clean. Whether an unconfigured clone's jobs come out **`skipped` rather than failed** is a GitHub *runtime* outcome that no check here executed — the documentation says a skipped dependency skips its dependents, which is the mechanism, but no run was observed. On owner instruction that outcome is now an **acceptance criterion** (B4) rather than an open unknown, and **U2** is restated as such. If the owner funds only one member, `claude.yml` alone is more defensible than `claude-code-review.yml` alone on spend, because only the review lane's spend is driven by repository traffic rather than by a human asking — but note that `claude.yml` is also the lane carrying the wider permission grant and the exposure analysed above, so the spend ranking and the security ranking point in opposite directions and the owner is choosing between them.

**What installing a credential costs the owner, and what an adopter must understand to accept it knowingly.** Two auditors declined to certify shipping this as revision 3 stated it, and the honest answer is not that they were wrong but that revision 3 asked the wrong question. Shipping the **files** and turning on the **capability** are separate acts with separate risk owners, and revision 3 conflated them. Revision 6 keeps that boundary and renames the second act: under the owner's design the act that turns the capability on is **installing a credential**, and it is taken by whoever installs it — **except where the credential was already there for another purpose, in which case no act is taken at all, and the paragraph after "Given (1) to (4)" below states that case.** Four facts fix the boundary:

1. **The template consumer is not exposed by the files alone.** With no credential configured — the state of every repository created from the template — no credentialed job runs, no credential exists, and no federation identifier is configured. The preflight job *does* run on a qualifying event: it reads five empty values, publishes `credential=none`, prints that the key needs setting, exits 0, and the real job is skipped. What a stranger receives is two files that announce what they need and a one-line way to refuse them, which is deleting the file. That defence rests on **B4**, which is now an acceptance criterion the implementation phase must prove rather than an open unknown, and if it fails a stranger gets a red check instead of a skipped job, which is precisely the `codex`/`doxa-openai` objection and a reason the runner-up would then win outright.
2. **The residual is a property of the capability, not of the files.** The runner-up does not remove it; it relocates the decision to adoption time. Anyone who follows the opt-in recipe inherits the same residual, because the residual is what the action does once it is running, not what the template ships. Under revision 6 the relocation is built in rather than documented elsewhere: the credential is the switch, and deleting the file is the refusal.
3. **The residual is not removable by configuration, and it binds both lanes.** A trusted author routinely quotes untrusted content, and no check separates the author's own words from text the author pasted. It is tempting to argue the review lane is safer on `contents: read`; the record does not support that, though it does not refute it either — and the distinction is the honest one. The S2 chain escalates through the **Claude GitHub App installation token**, not through the job's own grants (security-claims table, row 12). For the **assistant** lane that token's request is read in source and is write-capable: `additional_permissions` is set, so `token.ts:100` sends `DEFAULT_PERMISSIONS` merged with it — `contents`/`pull_requests`/`issues` `write`. For the **review** lane no body is sent at all (`token.ts:79-81`, `:115-118`), so the scope is chosen server-side and **is not readable from any source this research can reach** — that is **U17**, and *unknown is not read-only*. What is settled for both lanes is the input side: a pull-request diff written by a write-access author is trusted-author content on exactly the same footing as a comment, so the residual itself binds both. **Treat the review lane as write-capable until U17 says otherwise, rather than assuming it is not.**
4. **The pre-token window is real.** `setupGitHubToken()` runs before every actor-trust check inside the action (above, and E2/E3 in `action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`). The workflow's job-level `if:` is therefore the only control that acts before an App token is requested — and its runtime effect is **U12**, unproven.

Given (1) to (4): the exposure is **argued and bounded** — bounded by the fork guard, the bot guards on **both** lanes at revision 6, credential presence, the `author_association` prefilter, a pin above both fix versions, and a configuration outside every phase of the published chain — and it is **not eliminated**. Whether a bounded, unremovable exposure on a write-capable lane is acceptable is an owner risk judgement, not an engineering-fitness one. **Revision 6 does not soften that sentence and does not claim the judgement has been made for every repository.** What the owner settled on 2026-09-12 is the *mechanism* — no flag, credential presence, delete to refuse — and the consequence is that the judgement is taken per repository by whoever installs the credential, at the moment they install it. This decision therefore still declines to assert that the residual is acceptable; it states what accepting it means and puts that statement where the person installing the credential will see it. **An adopter accepting it knowingly must understand five things**, and they are written into the fixture headers because the fixture is what gets copied: that installing a credential on a public repository accepts an unremovable trusted-author prompt-injection residual; that a write-capable Claude GitHub App installation token is requested before any actor-trust check returns; that `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` is **inactive** in this configuration; that the `author_association` clause is a prefilter and not a permission check, and that the owner chose it over a write-permission check deliberately; and that the skipped-not-failed outcome for an unconfigured clone (B4) and the prefilter's effect (U12) are both unproven here, the first as an acceptance criterion P03 must prove and the second as an unproven item, and that both must be proved before a credential is installed anywhere public. **One case defeats the two sentences above** — the adopter whose repository already carries a credential for an unrelated purpose performs no act of acceptance and is shown nothing — **and it is stated next rather than left implicit.**

**The case this argument does not cover, stated here rather than left for an adopter to discover.** Everything above describes an adopter who **installs** a credential. A repository created from this template that **already holds `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` or `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` as a repository secret for an unrelated purpose** — both are the vendor's generic names, not names this template coined, and a Rust repository plausibly already runs some other Claude-invoking job — is in a different position, and three of the statements above are false for it. **No act of acceptance is taken**: the credential predates the files, so "installing a credential is itself the act of acceptance" attributes to an installer an act that nobody performed for these workflows. **Both lanes go live on the next qualifying event**: the detecting step reads the pre-existing secret, publishes `credential=static`, and each real job's gate `== 'static' || == 'federation'` is true. **And nothing prints**: the `::notice` annotation and the `$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY` block sit inside the `shape = none` branch of that step — lines **145-160** of each fixture as they now stand, `if [ "${shape}" = none ]; then` at 145 and `fi` at 160 in both `candidate-claude-code-review.r5.yml` and `candidate-claude.r5.yml`, with the `else` arm at 158-159 —, so on this path the step emits one line of ordinary log text and nothing else — which means the statement this decision places "where the person installing the credential will see it" reaches nobody on any run, and the stated opt-out, deleting the file, cannot be chosen by someone who does not know there is anything to refuse. The only surviving channel is the fixture header, which is the one file this adopter has no reason to open. **Who owns the residual in that case**: the residual is unchanged — facts (1) to (4) above describe it exactly — but it is taken **by default rather than by choice**, and it is owned by whoever created the repository from the template rather than by anyone who made a decision about these two files. This qualifies fact (2) above, "the credential is the switch, and deleting the file is the refusal", which is left byte-identical rather than edited because it sits among the residual facts; the qualification is that the switch may already be thrown and the refusal may never be offered. **This is also, concretely, how revision 6 exposes more than revision 5 downstream, and it is the larger of the two new costs.** Revision 5 required two conditions for a credentialed run — an enable variable set to `'true'` *and* a credential — and revision 6 requires one, so every repository state that ran under revision 5 runs under revision 6 and at least one state that did not now does. On the **origin repository** the difference is nil, because the flag was `true` by construction wherever the capability was wanted; the difference bites **downstream**. `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_REVIEW` and `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT` were dedicated consent acts that named the capability they switched on; credential presence is not. **The mechanism is the owner's 2026-09-12 direction and this decision neither changes it nor asks for it back.** Two things are recommended to P03 rather than implemented here: the acceptance criterion is **scoped** so that B4 proves this path as well as the no-credential path, and the `else` branch of the detecting step should **print which shape it detected and that deleting the file is the way to refuse** — a one-line change to a printed string, changing no gate and no permission, and the only channel that would reach this adopter at all.

**The unpinned marketplace fetch, adjudicated.** The empirical auditor named this and revision 3 did not address it; it is a real defect and revision 4 fixes it rather than routing it. What the source says at `v1.0.220` (`action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`, E5–E7):

| Fact | Where | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| `MARKETPLACE_URL_REGEX = /^https:\/\/[a-zA-Z0-9\-._~:/?#[\]@!$&'()*+,;=%]+\.git$/` anchors the **end** of the string to `.git` | `base-action/src/install-plugins.ts:7-8` | No ref, tag, commit or `#fragment` can be expressed. A marketplace URL is **structurally unpinnable** — not merely unpinned by choice |
| `addMarketplace` runs `["plugin", "marketplace", "add", marketplace]` | `install-plugins.ts:191-202` | The clone takes `anthropics/claude-code` at its **default branch**, `main`, whose `pushed_at` was `2026-09-10T06:06:11Z` at retrieval |
| The marketplace **name** `claude-code-plugins` and the plugin list are declared in `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` on that branch | `anthropics/claude-code@main` | Even the identifier the `plugins:` input binds to is remote mutable content |
| `installPlugins` is called at `base-action/src/index.ts:36`, **after** `setupWorkloadIdentity()` at `:18` | `base-action/src/index.ts` | The fetch happens inside the job that already holds the Anthropic credential and `id-token: write` |
| Neither `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` nor `examples/pr-review-comprehensive.yml` uses a marketplace; the maintainer runs `prompt: "/review-pr …"` against `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` in the action's **own tree** | `v1.0.220` | A pinned alternative exists in the maintainer's own reference and was available to revision 3 |

**Verdict: removed, not routed.** Pinning the action to `v1.0.220` pinned nothing about what the review lane actually executed, which defeats the purpose the pin was adopted for and contradicts architecture A's stated virtue — "YAML in `.github/workflows/`, versioned and reviewed like code". The replacement is the maintainer's own form: a template-local `.claude/commands/review-pr.md`, versioned with the template. **The cost is real and is stated rather than minimised**: the template owner now authors and maintains the review prompt instead of inheriting upstream's maintained `code-review` plugin, and P03 must write that file and its tool allowance before the lane does anything useful. An adopter who prefers the maintained plugin may restore the two inputs knowingly; that is a documented option, not the default. Only the **pin-form** question — that some run-time references cannot be pinned at all — is handed to **R20**; the configuration question is settled here. The two inputs came from `py`: `.github/workflows/claude-code-review.yml` at `b08bccfb` carries them at lines 49-51 alongside the `--comment` prompt (check 13), and revision 3 inherited them without adjudication — the same "`py` does it" failure the mandate exists to prevent, appearing in a decision that names that failure elsewhere.

**Ledger rows settled.** `F051` (AI-assisted PR code review workflow) and `F052` (AI assistant workflow triggered by @mention comments), `docs/port/COMMONALITY.md:57-58`, both `py-only`, both `DIVERGENT`, both bundled to R18. This decision recommends **shipping both files with the template by default, each lane gated on credential presence**, and is deliberately **not** an `ADOPT` — `docs/port/README.md:70` defines `ADOPT` as "one-sided precedent, language-neutral; taken as-is", and this recommendation does not take `py`'s configuration as-is: it raises one permission, handles two credential shapes where `py` hard-codes one static token, narrows one trigger list, adds a credential-presence preflight, adds a draft clause, adds an author-trust prefilter, adds bot exclusion to the assistant lane that `py` does not have, and — at revision 4 — **removes `py`'s run-time plugin-marketplace fetch**. The count of departures is now **eight**, where revision 5 counted seven: revision 5's "adds an enable flag" is withdrawn at revision 6 and replaced by the preflight, and the assistant lane's bot exclusion is the eighth. Assigning the final verdict and editing the ledger are the controller's, not this item's.

**Dates.** Every retrieval date in this decision is **UTC**. Revision 1's checks ran 2026-09-10 05:15–05:19Z; revision 2's five read-only re-runs were appended at 05:47:55Z and 05:51:18Z (all three host logs now carry mtime 05:55:07Z, later than either recorded append time; their content is unchanged against the committed tree, so the mtime moved without the bytes moving, the cause was not investigated, and revision 2's recorded times stand). Revision 3's checks ran 2026-09-10 06:27:19Z–06:29:17Z; each new log carries its own capture timestamp in its header, and the `actionlint.log` revision-3 addendum is stamped 06:29:17Z. Revision 4's checks were captured 2026-09-10 07:08:10Z–07:22:16Z. `action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log` carries `# captured: 2026-09-10T07:08:10Z` from its own `date -u` at the head of the file and has mtime 07:22:16Z, because the log was written in five appends, the last being the E9 correction block stamped 07:22:16Z by its own `date -u`; the two revision-4 fixtures carry mtimes 07:10:05Z and 07:10:27Z, and the `actionlint.log` revision-4 addendum is stamped 07:10:37Z by its own `date -u`, matching that file's mtime. Every timestamp is read from the file's mtime or from the command's own `date -u` output; none is estimated. Every timestamp is taken from the file's own mtime or from the command's own `date -u` output at capture, never estimated. The raw reports were retrieved 2026-09-06 (`codex`) and 2026-09-09 (`opus`, all three Doxa engines). **Revision 6's own checks ran 2026-09-12**: `candidate-claude-code-review.r5.yml` has mtime 18:25:38Z and `candidate-claude.r5.yml` 18:26:21Z; `needs-and-credential-shapes.log` carries `# captured: 2026-09-12T18:27:12Z` from its own `date -u` and mtime 18:27:13Z; the `actionlint.log` revision-6 addendum is stamped `2026-09-12T18:28:15Z` by its own `date -u`, its **second capture** — written after the fixtures' gate was corrected — is stamped `18:51:17Z`, and `protected-regions-r6.log` carries its own `date -u` header from the last capture of the five. The owner's amending direction is dated **2026-09-12**, and the always-run-preflight choice it implements was made **2026-09-11**.

### Principles and implementation

**Shared requirement, source, agreement level.** The requirement behind `py`'s two files is: *every pull request receives a first-pass automated review before a human reads it, and a maintainer can summon an agent into an issue or review thread without leaving GitHub.* Its source is the `py` implementation at pinned commit `b08bccfb55d05f15e46a83b52c5660b1881d19f5` (both files read verbatim, `source-repos-pinned.log`) plus ledger rows F051/F052.

The agreement level is **policy or mechanism**, the third row of the agreement-level table in the design spec §2 (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-01-rs-port-research-program-design.md`), which defines it as "Any deliberately shared value, protocol detail or tool, with its owner decision or evidence", varying in "Only the aspects outside that agreement; low-level choices can be shared when that level is intentional". The spec presents the three levels **unordered**, and no ranking among them is claimed here. Revision 2 called this level "the weakest of the three levels in `docs/port/README.md`"; that was wrong twice over and is withdrawn — `docs/port/README.md` mentions "agreement level" exactly once, at line 56, in a passing clause about A5 adjudication, and carries no list of the levels and no ranking; and spec §2's definition allows the *narrowest* variation of the three, not the widest.

The assignment is argued from what §2 says rather than from a rank. §2 requires that a policy-or-mechanism agreement carry "its owner decision or evidence". There is no owner decision registering these two workflows as a shared value, and the evidence in `## Context` shows the sources already disagree. **Checked again at revision 6, because the owner has now spoken on this item and the distinction matters**: the 2026-09-12 direction settles how the `rs` template gates these two files — no enable variable, credential presence, delete to refuse, bots rejected, association as the trust test. It does **not** register the pair as a value deliberately shared across `py`, `ts` and `rs`, and it says nothing about `ts`, which still ships neither file. The level assignment therefore stands unchanged, and so does the conclusion drawn from it in observation 1 below. Three observations place the requirement at this level rather than at "capability or standard" or "architectural pattern":

1. `ts-launch-blueprint` at `cb1cbcb2e88b898e8c081b0abbfabc1630079c00` ships eight workflow files, none AI-related, and zero paths under `.github/` matching `claude` (`source-repos-pinned.log`: the `ls-tree` listing the eight files exits **0**; the `ls-tree … | grep -ci claude` that returns the zero count exits **1**, which for `grep -c` *is* the zero-match result — see check 6). A capability-level or pattern-level agreement would already be violated by one of the three siblings; a policy-or-mechanism agreement without a registered owner decision is exactly what an unmatched sibling looks like.
2. Nothing in the capability is ecosystem-shaped, so there is no architectural pattern whose fitness in Rust could be the thing that must agree. The action reads a diff and posts comments; the diff being Rust changes no mechanism. There is no Rust analogue to select and no Rust idiom to honor.
3. The reviewing entity is a paid third-party service reached with an organization credential. Whether to spend it here needs "its owner decision or evidence" in §2's words, which is precisely why "`py` does it" cannot settle it.

**What must agree; what may vary.** What must agree across `py`, `ts` and `rs` is the **guard contract**, because it is simultaneously a security property and a correctness property and is ecosystem-neutral. It has four clauses, and clause (a) is scoped at revision 3 to the lane it actually binds:

- (a) **The review lane** — the only lane triggered by a `pull_request` event — never runs on a pull request from a fork, on the ground that a fork run cannot obtain the credential and could only fail. That is **a platform runtime behaviour that no check here executed**; the support available is the maintainer's own `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` at `v1.0.220`, which carries the identical guard with the comment "Skip on fork PRs since they can't mint the OIDC token used for Claude API auth" (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`), plus `py`'s own guard comment recording that the action "aborts on non-human actors … which showed as a **false red check** on the v2.4.1 release PR (#470)" and that "Skipping is a neutral (non-failing) outcome for this non-required job" (`source-repos-pinned.log`) — reference concordance and one field report, not an observed run (see **U3**). Revision 2 stated clause (a) as a property of the whole contract; that was inaccurate against this decision's own fixtures, and revision 3 corrects it. **The assistant lane cannot carry this clause and does not need it**: none of its four events is a `pull_request` event, so `GITHUB_REF` is the default branch, no fork ref is checked out, and the run happens in the base repository with its credentials by design. The assistant lane's fork-adjacent exposure is a *comment* on a fork pull request, which is `#1068`'s case and is answered by clause (d), not by a same-repository test.
- (b) Neither lane runs when either the triggering content's author or the run's initiator is a bot. **Revision 6 makes this clause true of both lanes, which it was not before, and the change closes a gap rather than restating a control.** Revisions 1 to 5 stated clause (b) as a property of the contract while only the review-lane fixture implemented it — `grep -c "type != 'Bot'"` over each fixture's YAML body returns **2** for every review-lane fixture and **0** for every assistant-lane fixture at revisions 1 to 5, against **5** for the revision-6 assistant fixture (check 21; the count is taken from the `name:` key onward, because the revision-6 headers discuss bot checks in comments and a whole-file count would score prose as configuration): that fixture carries `github.event.pull_request.user.type != 'Bot'` and `github.event.sender.type != 'Bot'`, and the assistant-lane fixture carried no bot check at all. The action does not close the gap downstream — `src/github/validation/permissions.ts:114` returns `true` unconditionally for any actor whose login ends in `[bot]` (check 10), and the refusal of App actors lives only in `checkHumanActor`, which runs after the App token is minted. On the owner's 2026-09-12 direction that both lanes must reject bots, the assistant lane now carries `github.event.sender.type != 'Bot'` plus the triggering content's own author type per event; the checks are type-based, not a name denylist, for the reason `## Engines` §2 gives. Lint-clean (`actionlint.log`, R6-1); runtime effect unproven and recorded as **B10**.
- (c) No credential is reachable from a run whose content an untrusted contributor controls.
- (d) **New at revision 3.** On a public repository the assistant lane never *starts* for an author outside `OWNER`, `MEMBER` or `COLLABORATOR`. This is a prefilter, not a permission check: `author_association` is not the same predicate as write access, and an organization `MEMBER` without write access passes it and is then refused by the action's own `checkWritePermissions` — which, **corrected at revision 4**, is called not by either mode but once in the shared entrypoint, `src/entrypoints/prepare.ts:44`, after `setupGitHubToken()` at `:37` (`action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`, E2). It admits every write-access holder — a repository collaborator reports `COLLABORATOR`, an organization member reports `MEMBER`, the owner reports `OWNER` — so it introduces no false refusal for a legitimate maintainer, and it is defence in depth in front of an authoritative action-side check, never a replacement for it. **Recorded at revision 6**: the owner considered replacing this prefilter with a genuine write-permission check on 2026-09-12 and chose `author_association` as the more accurate test, so clause (d) stays exactly as revision 3 wrote it and no write-permission check is added at the workflow level.

What may vary per repository: whether a credential is installed at all — or whether the file is deleted, which is the supported way to refuse the capability outright — which events are listened to, which model, and which prompt. **Restated at revision 6**: every revision from 1 to 5 wrote this as "whether the capability is enabled at all", which named a variable that no longer exists (check 21).

**Essential behaviors and observable acceptance criteria.**

| # | Essential behavior | Observable acceptance criterion (P03 proves each) |
|---|---|---|
| B1 | A qualifying pull-request event starts the review job | a human-authored PR in the configured origin repository produces a run with `conclusion: success` |
| B2 | Fork PRs, bot-authored PRs and bot-initiated runs are **skipped, not failed** | `.workflow_runs[0].conclusion == "skipped"` for each of: a fork PR, the release-please PR, a Dependabot PR |
| B3 | A run that starts actually posts its output | `gh api repos/<o>/<r>/issues/<pr>/comments --jq 'length'` ≥ 1; a green job with 0 comments reproduces `anthropics/claude-code-action#1121` |
| B4 | **Rewritten at revision 6, and promoted from an open unknown to an acceptance criterion on owner instruction.** A repository created from the template **with no credential configured** does not run either lane, does not error, and prints which key needs setting | for a qualifying PR and a qualifying `@claude` comment: the `credential-preflight` job's `conclusion == "success"` with output `credential=none`; the `claude-review` and `claude` jobs' `conclusion == "skipped"`; **no red check on the pull request**; and the run's step summary and annotations name `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY`, `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN`, `ANTHROPIC_FEDERATION_RULE_ID` and `ANTHROPIC_ORGANIZATION_ID`. Also assert the opt-out: with both files deleted, no run is created at all. The mechanism is documented — "If a job fails or is skipped, all jobs that need it are skipped" (`needs-and-credential-shapes.log`, N1) — and **unobserved**, which is why this is a criterion P03 must prove rather than a claim made here. **Scoped after the revision-6 judgment audit (JA-06), because the criterion above proves only the no-credential path.** P03 must also prove the **pre-existing-credential** path: in a repository that already holds `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` or `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` as a repository secret installed for an unrelated purpose *before* these two files land, and with no other configuration performed, assert that on each lane's next qualifying event the `credential-preflight` job publishes `credential=static`, that the lane's real job reaches `conclusion == "success"` rather than `"skipped"`, and that the run emits **no** `::notice` annotation and **no** `$GITHUB_STEP_SUMMARY` block — that is, that both lanes activate with nothing printed and no act of acceptance taken. That outcome is what the paragraph under "What installing a credential costs the owner" describes; P03 must show it is what actually happens, because it is the state in which the residual is taken by default |
| B5 | Only a write-access user can summon the assistant | the same `@claude` comment from a non-write account produces no agent action |
| B6 | The assistant can read CI results | `@claude why did the clippy job fail?` on a red PR yields a reply naming the failing job |
| B7 | **A commenter outside `OWNER`/`MEMBER`/`COLLABORATOR` does not start the assistant job at all** | an account with no association posts `@claude …` on the public origin repository; assert `.workflow_runs \| length == 0` for a run created by that event, or that **both jobs** report `conclusion == "skipped"` — **sharpened at revision 6**, because with the guards on the preflight the preflight is the job that is skipped, and asserting only its conclusion would pass while `claude` ran; assert no runner minutes are billed for it |
| B8 | **A pull request opened as a draft and later marked ready produces exactly one review run** | open a draft PR, mark it ready; assert the review workflow has exactly one run for that PR number |
| B9 | **New at revision 4. The review lane runs its instructions from the template's own tree, with no run-time marketplace fetch** | assert the run log contains `No marketplaces specified, skipping marketplace setup` (`base-action/src/install-plugins.ts:230`) and no `Adding marketplace:` line; and assert the run still posts, which requires P03 to have authored `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` **and** the tool allowance the maintainer's own reference carries as `claude_args: --allowedTools "mcp__github_inline_comment__create_inline_comment"`. This decision does not specify that prompt's content; **that the replacement posts at all is unproven here and is U18** |
| B10 | **New at revision 6. The assistant lane rejects bots**, which it did not do before this revision | post an `@claude …` comment from a bot account holding write access — the release-please App is the realistic one — on a repository that **has** a credential installed, because a repository without one proves nothing here. Assert either that no workflow run is created for that event or that **the `claude` job's** `conclusion == "skipped"`; asserting only that `credential-preflight` was skipped is **not sufficient**, because that assertion passes whether or not the real job ran. Then assert the same for an `issues: [assigned]` event where the assigner is a bot and the issue author is human, which is the case `github.event.sender.type` catches and the per-event author type does not. **Unproven here**: both checks lint clean and neither was run |
| B11 | **New at revision 6. Each credential shape is detected and used** | with only `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` set, assert the preflight prints `static` and the run authenticates; with only the two federation variables set, assert `federation` and that the run log carries the action's `Workload identity federation configured` line; with both set, assert the action's own `core.warning` about precedence appears and that the static credential is the one used (`needs-and-credential-shapes.log`, N2b) |

**Architectural alternatives, compared before libraries.** Four architectures can deliver "pull requests get an automated first-pass review"; the choice among them precedes the vendor choice.

| Architecture | Expressed as | Travels through "Use this template"? | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. Workflow-file agent (`claude-code-action`, `run-gemini-cli`, `codex-action`) | YAML in `.github/workflows/`, versioned and reviewed like code | **Yes** — the file is tree content | **Chosen.** The only architecture that is itself a template artifact. |
| B. Platform-configured reviewer (GitHub Copilot code review) | repository ruleset plus organization settings | No — repository *state*, not tree content | Rejected on fit: a template that ships nothing cannot transfer the capability, and the value test in the prompt is about two files. |
| C. Hosted third-party review app (PR-Agent as a service, CodeRabbit as an app) | a GitHub App installed on the repository | No | Rejected on the same fit ground, plus a second vendor relationship. |
| D. Self-hosted agent in a generic job | bespoke YAML plus a script | Yes | Rejected on maintenance cost: reimplements diff assembly, comment threading, deduplication and prompt-injection sanitization that A gets from a maintained action. |

Inside architecture A, `anthropics/claude-code-action` is selected over `The-PR-Agent/pr-agent`, `google-github-actions/run-gemini-cli` and `openai/codex-action` because it is the only candidate that (i) covers **both** members with one component, one credential and one App installation, (ii) is on a stable `v1` series with **no `v2` tag** among the 287 tag refs returned (`action-release.log`, HTTP 404 and `gh` exit 1 on `git/ref/tags/v2` — the correct result for a ref that does not exist), and (iii) has the maintainer running both shapes against its own repository in a form that can be read at a tag.

**Why the selected design preserves each principle.** Clause (a) is carried over from `py` byte-for-byte on the review lane, so it is preserved by construction there, and it is scoped to that lane for the reason given above. Clause (b) is carried over from `py` on the review lane and, **new at revision 6, is implemented on the assistant lane for the first time**. Clause (c) is preserved by credential presence downstream and by clause (d) upstream. The **additions** to `py`'s shape exist because `py`'s guard protects neither a template consumer nor a public origin repository:

*The credential-presence preflight, for the consumer — replacing the enable flag at revision 6.* In a repository created from the template, an internal PR satisfies `head.repo.full_name == github.repository`, so the fork guard passes and, without a further gate, the job would run with no credential. The platform bounds the available fix, and this is the constraint the whole design turns on: `jobs.<job_id>.if` may read only `github, needs, vars, inputs`; `secrets` is absent there and from `jobs.<job_id>.steps.if` as well, while it *is* available in `jobs.<job_id>.env` (verified in `contexts-availability.log`, and independently by `actionlint`, which rejects `if: secrets.X != ''` at the job level — **re-run for revision 6**, exit 1 with the quoted diagnostic, `actionlint.log` R6-2). **Revisions 1 to 5** took the one-line option the table allows and gated each job on a `vars` flag the repository owner had to set — all five, not only the later ones: the revision-1/2 fixtures carry `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_REVIEW == 'true'` and `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT == 'true'` at line 19 of each, as do the r3 and r4 fixtures, and every revision's own copy of this decision is read from git rather than from memory (check 21). **The owner's 2026-09-12 direction removes that flag, and the owner's 2026-09-11 choice names the replacement**: an always-run preflight job that prints the reason for skipping and gates the real job behind it. Because `secrets` is legal in `jobs.<job_id>.env` and `needs` is legal in `jobs.<job_id>.if`, the preflight can read either credential shape into environment values, print the skip reason, and publish a literal `static`/`federation`/`none` output that the real job gates on. Both fixtures lint clean, and `actionlint` **type-checks the relay** rather than merely parsing it: a deliberately misspelled `needs.credential-preflight.outputs.credentail` is rejected with `property "credentail" is not defined in object type {credential: string}` (`actionlint.log`, R6-4), so the wiring between the preflight's declared `outputs:` and the consumer's reference is verified. What the design intends is that both files are **silent and skipped, with a printed reason**, in every unconfigured downstream repository, and that a repository that wants neither deletes them. That intended effect is a design purpose plus a documented platform rule — a skipped dependency skips its dependents (`needs-and-credential-shapes.log`, N1) — and **not an observed run**: it is acceptance criterion **B4**. The step-level alternative revision 3 recorded (`env: HAS_CRED: ${{ secrets.X != '' }}` read by `steps.if`) is still legal under the same table and is still rejected for the same reason: a skipped *step* would sit inside a job reporting green, reading as a passing review that never happened. The preflight avoids that by putting the gate on a job boundary, where a skip is visible as a skip.

*The author-trust prefilter, for the origin repository.* `author_association` arrives through the `github` context, which the same context table lists as available in `jobs.<job_id>.if`, so `#1068`'s suggested fix is expressible at the job level; `actionlint` accepts the four-clause form with exit 0 (`actionlint.log`, revision-3 addendum, fixture `candidate-claude.r3.yml`), and accepts it again at revision 6 on the preflight job with the two bot type checks added (R6-1, fixture `candidate-claude.r5.yml`). The clause is written in the exact shape `#1068` asks for, `contains(fromJSON('["OWNER","MEMBER","COLLABORATOR"]'), github.event.<payload>.author_association)`, and `doxa-openai` independently asks for "a trusted association prefilter where practicable" (`raw/doxa-openai.normalized.md:388`). What the prefilter does **not** do is stated with it, three times over: it is not a write-access check (clause (d) above); it does not stop the run for an OWNER, MEMBER or COLLABORATOR who pastes untrusted text, which is the residual carried into the runner-up's third condition and restated at revision 6 with its force unchanged; and it would **not** have stopped the published chain's Phase 2, whose triggering comment came from a trusted author and whose body was tampered with afterwards — that vector is closed instead by the action's own `isBodySafeToUse` at `v1.0.220`, read in source (`action-guard-source-v1.0.220.log`, G4).

*What guards the origin repository, in order, and where each one lives.* This list exists because revision 2 rested the security case on one endpoint returning one row, and because only the last entry is in this decision's gift.

| Layer | Where it lives | Read at `v1.0.220`? | What it does not do |
|---|---|---|---|
| `author_association` prefilter | the workflow, clause (d) | expressible and lint-clean; **runtime effect is U12** | not a write-access check; no help against a trusted author quoting untrusted text |
| **Bot type checks — added at revision 6 on the assistant lane, where revision 5 had none** | the workflow, clause (b), on the `credential-preflight` job of both lanes | expressible and lint-clean (R6-1); **runtime effect is B10**, not observed | nothing against a human actor; and it is a *workflow*-level check, so it cannot help a repository that deletes it. It exists because `permissions.ts:114` returns `true` for every `[bot]` login, so the action's write check is not the layer that stops a bot |
| `checkHumanActor` | `src/github/validation/actor.ts`, called **per mode** at `src/modes/tag/index.ts:43` and `src/modes/agent/index.ts:30` — and, corrected at revision 4, **after** the trigger check and **after** the App token is minted | **yes**, G3; call sites re-read at revision 4 (E2) | consults `allowed_bots`, which this decision leaves `""` |
| `checkWritePermissions` | `src/github/validation/permissions.ts`, called **once in the shared entrypoint** at `src/entrypoints/prepare.ts:44` — **not** inside either mode (`grep -c` returns 0 in both mode files) — and after `setupGitHubToken()` at `:37` | **yes**, G1; topology corrected at revision 4 (E2) | **returns `true` unconditionally for any actor whose login ends in `[bot]`** (line 114); the refusal of App actors lives entirely in `checkHumanActor`, so these are two layers where the published bypass needed only one to fail |
| `setupGitHubToken` — **added at revision 4, and it is not a guard** | `src/github/token.ts:158`, called first at `src/entrypoints/prepare.ts:37` | **yes**, E3 | it mints rather than checks: it exchanges the OIDC token for a Claude GitHub App installation token (`token.ts:121`), requesting `contents`/`pull_requests`/`issues` `write` plus any `additional_permissions` (`token.ts:69-73`, `:100`), **before** either check above runs |
| `allowed_bots: ""`, `allowed_non_write_users: ""` | `action.yml` defaults | yes (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`) | `docs/security.md` warns that allowed bots "are not checked for repository permissions" — relevant only if a later revision sets them |
| `isBodySafeToUse` | `src/github/data/fetcher.ts:312` | **yes**, G4 | falls open when no trigger timestamp is available (`if (!triggerTime) return true`) |
| `show_full_output: false` | `docs/security.md:181` | yes, G5 | disables the run-summary channel only |
| `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` — **available at the tag but INACTIVE in this configuration** (empirical finding F2, corrected at revision 4) | `action.yml:301` emits it only as `${{ env.CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB \|\| (inputs.allowed_non_write_users != '' && '1') \|\| '' }}`; the `bubblewrap`/`socat` install at `action.yml:228` is gated `inputs.allowed_non_write_users != '' && runner.os == 'Linux'` | yes, read at the tag (E4) — but **it does not fire here** | this decision leaves `allowed_non_write_users` at `""` and neither fixture sets the `env` variable, so **the flag is empty and the isolation dependencies are not installed**. It cannot be counted as an effective control in this configuration. Even where it does fire, `docs/security.md:16` calls it "best-effort" and says it "reduces but does not eliminate prompt injection risk" |

**Reference implementations, read at a pinned tag.** All at `anthropics/claude-code-action` `v1.0.220`, retrieved 2026-09-10 (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`, `action-guard-source-v1.0.220.log`): the maintainer's own `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` (`types: [opened]` only, `contents: read` / `pull-requests: write` / `id-token: write`, federation via `vars.*`, `actions/checkout@v6`); the maintainer's own `.github/workflows/claude.yml` (four events, four-clause `@claude` condition, `contents: write` / `pull-requests: write` / `issues: write` / `id-token: write`, federation via `vars.*`, and `uses: …@main` — dogfooding, **not** a pattern to copy into a template); `examples/pr-review-comprehensive.yml` (`pull-requests: write`); `examples/claude.yml` (whose `on:` and `if:` blocks are **character-identical** to `py`'s `claude.yml`); `examples/claude-wif.yml`. **None of these five carries an `author_association` prefilter**, which is what `#1068` is about; reference concordance therefore corroborates the trigger list and the permission set and says nothing in favour of the missing gate, and is not treated as if it did. The `code-review@claude-code-plugins` plugin `py` invokes exists (`anthropics/claude-code` `contents/plugins/code-review` returns `.claude-plugin`, `README.md`, `commands`), and **revision 4 removes it from the recommendation** — see "The unpinned marketplace fetch, adjudicated" above, where the trust edge `opus` records (`raw/opus.md:589`) is decided rather than handed on. The maintainer's own review reference is now doubly load-bearing: it not only corroborates the trigger list and the permission set but supplies the pinned prompt form that replaces the marketplace fetch — `prompt: "/review-pr REPO: … PR_NUMBER: …"` at `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml:32`, resolving to `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` in the action's own tree (E7). Neither that workflow nor `examples/pr-review-comprehensive.yml` passes `plugin_marketplaces` or `plugins` at all (`grep -c` returns 0 for both).

**Maturity, dependability and integration cost.** `anthropics/claude-code-action`: `stargazers_count` 8833, `archived: false`, `pushed_at` 2026-09-09T20:12:20Z, `license.spdx_id: MIT`, created 2025-05-19 (`action-release.log`). Newest non-prerelease `v1.0.220`, published 2026-09-09T20:12:09Z; `v1.0.212`→`v1.0.220` is 8 patch releases in 8 days, so **cadence ≈ one patch per day**. Cross-ecosystem adoption: `search/code` for the action under `.github/workflows` returns `total_count: 20480`. Rust-ecosystem adoption: `org:rust-lang` returns `total_count: 0`. Maintenance state by the rubric: **active** — a release on the retrieval date makes the six-month trigger unreachable.

The negative signal is **triage, and it is now stated with the figure two engines computed rather than deferred**: 457 open issues, and of the ten most recently opened issues **zero received a first response from an `OWNER`, `MEMBER` or `COLLABORATOR`; unanswered = 10; median time to first maintainer response is not computable from a sample with no responses**. `opus` computed this over `#1818, #1817, #1798, #1796, #1795, #1788, #1781, #1779, #1777, #1776` (created 2026-09-02 to 2026-09-09), noting `#1817` accumulated 12 community comments with no maintainer reply (`raw/opus.md:167`); `codex` computed the same result independently on 2026-09-06 over its own ten (`raw/codex.md:91`). Both samples were days old at retrieval, so neither supports an `at-risk` verdict on its own. The sharper datum is `#1068` itself: the maintainer's own labels mark it `p1` and `area:permissions`, and it has stood open with zero comments for **six months** (2026-03-18 → 2026-09-10) — a security-relevant, self-triaged priority-one issue that no maintainer has answered. That is the dependability figure a consumer of this action should weigh, and revision 2 recorded it as a comment count. Integration cost is one YAML file per member plus one Anthropic Console setup; there is **no per-run cost, token or latency figure** — `#1482` ("Feature request: expose per-run cost and token usage as Action outputs", open since 2026-07-08) is the evidence that the figure is not obtainable, so cost is reasoned structurally and recorded as a gap, never estimated.

**Fitness gates, answered before popularity.** No candidate fails a gate. Both members are GitHub Actions workflows, not Rust crates, so the crate-shaped figures are `inapplicable` with the reason stated rather than omitted: **90-day and all-time downloads** — inapplicable, crates.io has no record of a GitHub Action; **RustSec advisories** — inapplicable, RustSec indexes crates.io packages. (1) **License** — pass: `MIT`, compatible with `MIT OR Apache-2.0`, and invoking an action propagates no license term into the template's artifacts. (2) **MSRV** — inapplicable: a composite action (`runs.using: "composite"`) implemented in TypeScript on Bun; it never enters `Cargo.toml` or the dependency tree, so `msrv-policy` is not engaged.

**(3) Advisory and `unsafe` posture — rewritten at revision 3, because revision 2's version of this gate was wrong about the security history.** The RustSec half is inapplicable. The GitHub advisory database analogue is applicable, was checked, and is **partial**: `repos/anthropics/claude-code-action/security-advisories` returns exactly one published advisory, and revision 2 presented that single row as the security history. It is not. The record has at least two distinct entries:

| # | What | Severity | Disclosed | Fixed | GitHub advisory? | Exploited in the wild? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 | `GHSA-8q5r-mmjf-575q` / `CVE-2026-47751` — malicious `.mcp.json` in an attacker-controlled PR head plus `enableAllProjectMcpServers` → arbitrary code execution on the runner and exfiltration of workflow secrets | `medium` | published 2026-05-20 | `1.0.74` | **yes** | not stated |
| S2 | A permission-bypass plus indirect-prompt-injection chain disclosed by RyotaK of GMO Flatt Security: a GitHub App installation token bypasses `checkWritePermissions`, an issue-triage workflow with `allowed_non_write_users: "*"` yields an `issues: write` token, and that token tampers with a trusted user's issue body so a **tag-mode** run exfiltrates `ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN_REQUEST_TOKEN` / `_URL` and exchanges them for a Claude GitHub App installation token with full write access | Anthropic "rated these vulnerabilities **7.8 under CVSS v4.0**" and paid $3,800 + a $1,000 bypass bonus | published 2026-06-01; reported 2026-01-12 and 2026-01-17 | "fixed them as of Claude Code GitHub Actions **v1.0.94**" | **no** | **yes** — "Variants of the misconfiguration issues described in this article were actively exploited in the wild before this article was published"; the timeline records a similar misconfiguration in Cline's GitHub Actions exploited in the wild on **2026-02-17** |

S2's source is <https://flatt.tech/research/posts/poisoning-claude-code-one-github-issue-to-break-the-supply-chain/>, HTTP 200, retrieved 2026-09-10 UTC (`flatt-disclosure-2026-06-01.log`). **`doxa-gemini` cited it and revision 2 dismissed it on a category error.** Revision 2 wrote that gemini's "patched definitively in `v1.0.94`" claim "does not match the record" because the one published advisory was patched in `1.0.74` and because `v1.0.94`'s release notes describe a `PATH` change for `allowed_non_write_users`. Both comparisons were against the wrong object: gemini's claim is about **S2**, not S1, and "fixed as of v1.0.94" is a statement about the cumulative fixed state, not about one release's changelog. Read against its own primary source, **gemini's version figure is corroborated, not refuted**, and gemini's characterisation — "sophisticated bot accounts could successfully spoof authorization mechanisms and execute arbitrary prompt injections to steal OpenID Connect tokens" (`raw/doxa-gemini.normalized.md:185`) — is an accurate summary of S2. What stays rejected is gemini's **pin**: `v1.0.175` is `py`'s pin and 45 patch releases behind current, and currency, not safety, is the ground (see `## Engines` §3).

*Is the recommended configuration exposed to S2?* Phase by phase, and the answer is no as published, with the residual named:

- **Phase 1** needs a second workflow with `allowed_non_write_users: "*"` and `issues: write` — `examples/issue-triage.yml`. This decision recommends no such workflow and leaves `allowed_non_write_users` at its `""` default (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`). **Not present.**
- **Phase 2** needs (i) a tag-mode workflow with `id-token: write` — the recommended assistant lane *is* exactly that shape, and the article's reproduction of "the default workflow created when you set up Claude Code GitHub Actions" has an `on:` block and a four-clause `@claude` `if:` character-identical to `py`'s and to the revision-2 fixture; (ii) an `issues: write` token obtained in Phase 1, unavailable without it; and (iii) the ability to edit the triggering body after the trigger, which `isBodySafeToUse` closes at `v1.0.220` (G4). **The shape is present; the chain is not.**
- **The App-trigger bypass** that Phase 1 originally relied on is closed by `checkHumanActor`, which both modes call at `v1.0.220` (G3) — with the residual, read in source and recorded above, that `checkWritePermissions` alone still returns `true` for any `[bot]`-suffixed actor.
- **The recommended pin is above both fix versions**: `v1.0.220` > `1.0.94` > `1.0.74`. `py`'s `v1.0.175` is also above both, so the earlier reading that `py` is safe on this axis stands.
- **What is not closed** is the class. `docs/security.md` at `v1.0.220` describes prompt-injection sanitization and says plainly that "new bypass techniques may emerge", and revision 3 records the provenance the auditor asked for: several defaults this decision reads as design features are **incident remediations from S2** — the App-trigger refusal, the disabled run-summary channel, the subprocess environment scrub, and the edited-body rule. Knowing that, `docs/security.md`'s assurances are weighed as a maintainer's account of fixes already forced, not as evidence that the surface was designed safe.
- **Corrected at revision 4 (empirical finding F2): three of those four remediations are active in the recommended configuration; the subprocess environment scrub is not.** Revision 3 wrote that the four were "present at the pin" and used the set as evidence for the recommendation. Present at the pin and active in this configuration are different claims. `action.yml:301` emits `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` as `${{ env.CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB || (inputs.allowed_non_write_users != '' && '1') || '' }}`, and the step that installs its isolation dependencies, `action.yml:228`, is gated `inputs.allowed_non_write_users != '' && runner.os == 'Linux'`. This decision leaves `allowed_non_write_users` at its `""` default and neither fixture sets the `env` variable, so the flag evaluates to the empty string and `bubblewrap`/`socat` are never installed (`action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`, E4). **The scrub is therefore recorded as available but inactive, and is not counted as a control here.** This does not restore S2 Phase 1 — that needs `allowed_non_write_users: "*"`, which is the same default keeping the scrub off. **The decision declines to activate it**, because the only workflow-side activation path is setting `env: CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB: '1'`, which satisfies `:301` but **not** `:228`, producing the flag without the isolation dependencies it names; the effect of that half-state is not readable from the source and is recorded as **U16** for P03. Revision 3 held the fact that refutes its own claim: fitness gate 4 already recorded that all three `runner.os` guards are unreached because `allowed_non_write_users` is `""`, and `action.yml:228` is gated on exactly that expression.
