## Supersedes

**Revision 5, published 2026-09-10, sha256 `b45a17a342bfa49689c3571e71c14ab55c2fa64e0f9337032876739babdae205`, superseded 2026-09-12 by owner direction.** This section exists because revision 6 **reverses part of a published decision** — it removes `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_REVIEW` and `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT`, which revision 5 recommended, and dissolves the enablement condition revision 5 carried — and the approved spec permits a `## Supersedes` H2 for exactly that case. It is the only H2 beyond `## Decision`, `## Parameters`, `## Empirical check` and `## Engines`, and it sits last so those four remain in order before it, with `re-verify:` still the final line of `## Decision`.

**What revision 6 reversed**, in one line each: both files now ship by default with **no enable variable**; each lane is gated on **credential presence** through an always-run preflight that prints the missing key; **deleting the file** is the opt-out; the **assistant lane rejects bots**, which revision 5's fixture did not; the `author_association` prefilter is **unchanged** by deliberate owner choice; **both credential shapes** are handled rather than federation alone; and the unproven inertness of an unconfigured clone becomes **acceptance criterion B4**. What revision 6 did **not** change — the pin, the permission blocks, the trigger lists, the residual analysis and the four fixed parameter lines — is established by diff against the bytes preserved below, in `## Empirical check` check 20.

The complete revision-5 entry follows, preserved verbatim.

# R18 — ai-assisted-review-workflows (bundle, Deep) — decision (revision 5, 2026-09-10)

## Decision

**Status.** This decision is a **proposal to be validated in the implementation phase against the real template**. `rs-launch-blueprint` has no `.github/workflows/` tree, no Rust code and no configured credential today; P02 is research and P03 builds the template, and P03's CI is what proves or disproves what is recorded here. A recommendation that does not survive implementation is **revised against this recorded research**, not researched again from scratch. Nothing below claims owner acceptance: the owner has reviewed R01 only.

**Recommendation — moved at revision 4, and the move is the condition.** Ship **both** workflow files, as **one bundle**, from `anthropics/claude-code-action` at **`v1.0.220`** (annotated tag `020e931323e467e00b803ad64e03410d3796b8f7` → commit `19dda84776b3518d98b8798e591daee763049ed3`, resolved 2026-09-10), **as inert artifacts**, and **do not enable either lane on any public repository — the origin repository included — until the owner has recorded acceptance of the residual named under "What enabling costs the owner" below.** Revisions 1 to 3 recommended shipping both without qualification and treated origin-repository enablement as settled; it is not settled, no owner acceptance is on record (the owner has reviewed R01 only), and revision 4 makes the condition the recommendation rather than a footnote. **If the owner withholds that acceptance, the documented runner-up is what ships**: delete both files and publish the opt-in recipe. Also new at revision 4: **the run-time plugin-marketplace fetch is removed** from the review lane, on evidence read at the tag (see "The unpinned marketplace fetch, adjudicated" below). Carried unchanged from revision 3: `pull-requests: write` on the review job, **workload identity federation** as the credential path, the author-trust prefilter on the assistant lane, and the `draft == false` clause on the review lane. That an unconfigured repository yields a `skipped` job rather than a red check remains **U2**, unproven here.

| File | Trigger | Job-level guard | Permissions | Action inputs |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `.github/workflows/claude-code-review.yml` | `pull_request: types: [opened, ready_for_review, reopened]` | `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_REVIEW == 'true'` **and** `py`'s fork guard **and** both `py` bot guards, unchanged **and** `github.event.pull_request.draft == false` (**new at revision 3**) | `contents: read`, `pull-requests: write`, `issues: read`, `id-token: write` | three `anthropic_federation_*` identifiers from `vars.*`; `prompt: '/review-pr REPO: … PR_NUMBER: …'`, resolving from a **template-local** `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` that P03 must author. **`plugin_marketplaces` and `plugins` are removed at revision 4** — revision 3 passed `plugin_marketplaces: 'https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code.git'` and `plugins: 'code-review@claude-code-plugins'`, which made this lane's instructions unpinnable |
| `.github/workflows/claude.yml` | `issue_comment: [created]`, `pull_request_review_comment: [created]`, `issues: [opened, assigned]`, `pull_request_review: [submitted]` | `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT == 'true'` **and** `py`'s four-clause `contains(…, '@claude')` condition **and**, on each of those four clauses, `contains(fromJSON('["OWNER","MEMBER","COLLABORATOR"]'), github.event.<comment\|review\|issue>.author_association)` (**new at revision 3**) | `contents: write`, `pull-requests: write`, `issues: write`, `id-token: write`, `actions: read` | the same three federation identifiers; `additional_permissions: actions: read`; **no** `prompt:` input (omitting it selects interactive mode — `docs/faq.md` at `v1.0.220`: "**Without `prompt` input**: Runs in interactive mode - waits for @claude mentions in comments", `action-docs-quotes-v1.0.220.log`) |

**Why the assistant lane gains an author-trust prefilter, stated here because it is the security case.** The `vars` enable flag protects a **downstream fork**, where it is unset. It cannot protect the **origin repository**, where it is `true` by construction, the federation identifiers are configured, and strangers can comment on a public template. Upstream issue `anthropics/claude-code-action#1068` — "Default workflow template missing author trust gate — secrets exposed on public repos", open since 2026-03-18, labels `bug`, `documentation`, `area:permissions`, `p1`, author `NONE`, 0 comments, `updated_at` 44 seconds after creation (`issue-1068.log`) — names three exposures of the unprefiltered `if:` and asks for exactly this clause. Revision 2 recorded that issue only as a comment count. Read in full, its three exposures divide as follows against the recommended configuration:

| `#1068`'s exposure | Answered by federation? | Answered by the enable flag on the origin repository? | Answered by the revision-3 prefilter? |
|---|---|---|---|
| The job starts and consumes runner minutes | No | No | **Yes**, for any commenter outside `OWNER`/`MEMBER`/`COLLABORATOR` — **U12**, not observed |
| `secrets.ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` / `secrets.CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` reach the runner | **Yes** — there is no such secret under federation | No | Moot under federation |
| An OIDC token is obtainable (`id-token: write`) | **No** | No | **Yes**, same qualification |

The third row is the correction that matters, and it revises a claim `opus` made and revision 2 carried: federation was recorded as a mitigation for `#1068`, and it answers only the second row. `ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN_REQUEST_TOKEN` and `ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN_REQUEST_URL` are present in the runner environment from the moment a job with `id-token: write` starts, and the 2026-06-01 GMO Flatt Security disclosure describes them as "the credentials required to request that OIDC token, so an attacker who obtains them can replicate the entire token exchange process" — the exchange whose output is a Claude GitHub App installation token with write access to contents, issues, pull requests and workflows (`flatt-disclosure-2026-06-01.log`). Federation changes which credential is worth stealing; it does not remove the one the published chain actually steals. **Scoped at revision 4, because revision 3 overstated it (empirical finding F3).** Revision 3 said the prefilter "is the only control in this record that acts before the job starts". That is wrong as written: the assistant lane's job-level `if:` also carries `vars.ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT == 'true'`, and a `vars` clause is pre-job control too. The supported claim is the narrower one: **once the origin repository sets `ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT` to `true`, the `author_association` clause is the only actor-trust control that acts before the job is scheduled** — the enable flag is a capability switch, not an actor check, and it is `true` by construction on the repository this decision is about. That is why the prefilter is adopted rather than offered.

**And it is load-bearing for a reason revision 3 did not have, found at revision 4 while correcting F1.** `src/entrypoints/prepare.ts:37` calls `setupGitHubToken()` **before** the write-permission check at `:44` and before either mode calls `checkHumanActor`. `setupGitHubToken()` exchanges the workflow's OIDC token for a Claude GitHub App installation token at `https://api.anthropic.com/api/github/github-app-token-exchange` (`src/github/token.ts:121`). Because the assistant lane passes `additional_permissions`, `parseAdditionalPermissions()` returns a value, so `token.ts:100` sends `DEFAULT_PERMISSIONS` merged with it — `contents: "write"`, `pull_requests: "write"`, `issues: "write"` (`token.ts:69-73`) plus `actions: read`. **A write-capable App installation token is therefore requested before any actor-trust check inside the action returns** (`action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`, E2 and E3). Whether anything attacker-controlled is processed in that window was not traced; that is **U14**. On the review lane `additional_permissions` is unset, `parseAdditionalPermissions()` returns `undefined`, no body is sent, and the resulting permission set is chosen server-side and is **not readable from this source** — recorded as **U17**.

**Ranked runner-up, promoted at revision 4 from "ranked second" to "the default if acceptance is withheld".** Ship **neither** workflow and publish a documented opt-in recipe instead (the `codex` and `doxa-openai` position). It wins on any of three owner judgements, and revision 4 changes its standing rather than its content: because the third condition below is now *live and unresolved* rather than hypothetical, the runner-up is no longer merely an alternative the owner may pick. Two things follow, and they are different: while acceptance is unrecorded the shipped state — two files with their variables unset — is **functionally equivalent** to the runner-up, because nothing runs either way; and if acceptance is **withheld**, the runner-up is what ships, meaning the two files are deleted and only the recipe remains. Two of its three conditions are the ones revision 2 recorded: the owner declines recurring third-party spend on the origin repository, or declines shipping a vendor-AI exemplar inside a public template tree. **The third is added at revision 3 and is the one the judgment audit asked to see argued**: the owner declines the residual prompt-injection exposure of a **write-capable assistant lane on a public repository, reachable from text an OWNER, MEMBER or COLLABORATOR writes** — because a trusted author routinely quotes untrusted content (pasting an issue body, a fork pull request's diff, a stack trace) into a comment, and neither the prefilter nor any action-side check distinguishes a trusted author's own words from untrusted text that author pasted. The researcher who published the chain states the general form of that residual plainly: "The permission model around Claude Code isn't hardened enough to safely handle untrusted input… at the time of writing, I have reported around 50 separate vulnerabilities to Anthropic that allow attackers to bypass the permission system and execute arbitrary commands in Claude Code" (`flatt-disclosure-2026-06-01.log`). That residual is **not** the published chain — see the exposure analysis under fitness gate 3, which finds the recommended configuration outside every phase of it — but it is a live class, and it is an owner risk decision, not an engineering-fitness one.

The runner-up's *other* stated grounds — that a public template cannot supply a downstream consumer's credential, and that an unconfigured fork would get a red check — are what the enable flag is **designed** to answer, and the evidence gathered here supports that design **syntactically, not yet at runtime**. Precisely what the checks establish (see `## Empirical check`, `contexts-availability.log` and `actionlint.log`): a job-level `if:` **may** reference the `vars` and `github` contexts and **may not** reference `secrets`, so both a credential-independent enable flag and an `author_association` prefilter are expressible at the job level at all. Whether an unset variable makes the job **`skipped` rather than failed** is a GitHub *runtime* outcome that no check here executed; it is **U2**. If the owner funds only one member, `claude.yml` alone is more defensible than `claude-code-review.yml` alone on spend, because only the review lane's spend is driven by repository traffic rather than by a human asking — but note that `claude.yml` is also the lane carrying the wider permission grant and the exposure analysed above, so the spend ranking and the security ranking point in opposite directions and the owner is choosing between them.

**What enabling costs the owner, and what an adopter must understand to accept it knowingly.** Two auditors declined to certify shipping this as revision 3 stated it, and the honest answer is not that they were wrong but that revision 3 asked the wrong question. Shipping the **files** and enabling the **capability** are separate acts with separate risk owners, and revision 3 conflated them. Four facts fix the boundary:

1. **The template consumer is not exposed by the files alone.** With `ENABLE_CLAUDE_REVIEW` and `ENABLE_CLAUDE_ASSISTANT` unset — the state of every repository created from the template — no job runs, no credential exists, and no federation identifier is configured. What a stranger receives is two inert files plus a documented enablement decision. That defence rests entirely on **U2**, which is unproven, and if U2 fails a stranger gets a red check instead of an inert file, which is precisely the `codex`/`doxa-openai` objection and a reason the runner-up would then win outright.
2. **The residual is a property of the capability, not of the files.** The runner-up does not remove it; it relocates the decision to adoption time. Anyone who follows the opt-in recipe inherits the same residual, because the residual is what the action does once it is running, not what the template ships.
3. **The residual is not removable by configuration, and it binds both lanes.** A trusted author routinely quotes untrusted content, and no check separates the author's own words from text the author pasted. It is tempting to argue the review lane is safer on `contents: read`; the record does not support that, though it does not refute it either — and the distinction is the honest one. The S2 chain escalates through the **Claude GitHub App installation token**, not through the job's own grants (security-claims table, row 12). For the **assistant** lane that token's request is read in source and is write-capable: `additional_permissions` is set, so `token.ts:100` sends `DEFAULT_PERMISSIONS` merged with it — `contents`/`pull_requests`/`issues` `write`. For the **review** lane no body is sent at all (`token.ts:79-81`, `:115-118`), so the scope is chosen server-side and **is not readable from any source this research can reach** — that is **U17**, and *unknown is not read-only*. What is settled for both lanes is the input side: a pull-request diff written by a write-access author is trusted-author content on exactly the same footing as a comment, so the residual itself binds both. **Treat the review lane as write-capable until U17 says otherwise, rather than assuming it is not.**
4. **The pre-token window is real.** `setupGitHubToken()` runs before every actor-trust check inside the action (above, and E2/E3 in `action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`). The workflow's job-level `if:` is therefore the only control that acts before an App token is requested — and its runtime effect is **U12**, unproven.

Given (1) to (4): the exposure is **argued and bounded** — bounded by the fork guard, the bot guards, the enable flag, the `author_association` prefilter, a pin above both fix versions, and a configuration outside every phase of the published chain — and it is **not eliminated**. Whether a bounded, unremovable exposure on a write-capable lane is acceptable is an owner risk judgement, not an engineering-fitness one, and this decision does not have that judgement on record. So it does not assert one. **An adopter accepting it knowingly must understand five things**, and they are written into the fixture headers because the fixture is what gets copied: that enabling on a public repository accepts an unremovable trusted-author prompt-injection residual; that a write-capable Claude GitHub App installation token is requested before any actor-trust check returns; that `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` is **inactive** in this configuration; that the `author_association` clause is a prefilter and not a permission check; and that both the inertness of an unconfigured fork (U2) and the prefilter's effect (U12) are unproven and P03 must prove them before the variable is flipped anywhere public.

**The unpinned marketplace fetch, adjudicated.** The empirical auditor named this and revision 3 did not address it; it is a real defect and revision 4 fixes it rather than routing it. What the source says at `v1.0.220` (`action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`, E5–E7):

| Fact | Where | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| `MARKETPLACE_URL_REGEX = /^https:\/\/[a-zA-Z0-9\-._~:/?#[\]@!$&'()*+,;=%]+\.git$/` anchors the **end** of the string to `.git` | `base-action/src/install-plugins.ts:7-8` | No ref, tag, commit or `#fragment` can be expressed. A marketplace URL is **structurally unpinnable** — not merely unpinned by choice |
| `addMarketplace` runs `["plugin", "marketplace", "add", marketplace]` | `install-plugins.ts:191-202` | The clone takes `anthropics/claude-code` at its **default branch**, `main`, whose `pushed_at` was `2026-09-10T06:06:11Z` at retrieval |
| The marketplace **name** `claude-code-plugins` and the plugin list are declared in `.claude-plugin/marketplace.json` on that branch | `anthropics/claude-code@main` | Even the identifier the `plugins:` input binds to is remote mutable content |
| `installPlugins` is called at `base-action/src/index.ts:36`, **after** `setupWorkloadIdentity()` at `:18` | `base-action/src/index.ts` | The fetch happens inside the job that already holds the Anthropic credential and `id-token: write` |
| Neither `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` nor `examples/pr-review-comprehensive.yml` uses a marketplace; the maintainer runs `prompt: "/review-pr …"` against `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` in the action's **own tree** | `v1.0.220` | A pinned alternative exists in the maintainer's own reference and was available to revision 3 |

**Verdict: removed, not routed.** Pinning the action to `v1.0.220` pinned nothing about what the review lane actually executed, which defeats the purpose the pin was adopted for and contradicts architecture A's stated virtue — "YAML in `.github/workflows/`, versioned and reviewed like code". The replacement is the maintainer's own form: a template-local `.claude/commands/review-pr.md`, versioned with the template. **The cost is real and is stated rather than minimised**: the template owner now authors and maintains the review prompt instead of inheriting upstream's maintained `code-review` plugin, and P03 must write that file and its tool allowance before the lane does anything useful. An adopter who prefers the maintained plugin may restore the two inputs knowingly; that is a documented option, not the default. Only the **pin-form** question — that some run-time references cannot be pinned at all — is handed to **R20**; the configuration question is settled here. The two inputs came from `py`: `.github/workflows/claude-code-review.yml` at `b08bccfb` carries them at lines 49-51 alongside the `--comment` prompt (check 13), and revision 3 inherited them without adjudication — the same "`py` does it" failure the mandate exists to prevent, appearing in a decision that names that failure elsewhere.

**Ledger rows settled.** `F051` (AI-assisted PR code review workflow) and `F052` (AI assistant workflow triggered by @mention comments), `docs/port/COMMONALITY.md:57-58`, both `py-only`, both `DIVERGENT`, both bundled to R18. This decision recommends **shipping both files as inert artifacts, with enablement conditional as stated above**, and is deliberately **not** an `ADOPT` — `docs/port/README.md:70` defines `ADOPT` as "one-sided precedent, language-neutral; taken as-is", and this recommendation does not take `py`'s configuration as-is: it raises one permission, changes the credential path, narrows one trigger list, adds an enable flag, adds a draft clause, adds an author-trust prefilter and — at revision 4 — **removes `py`'s run-time plugin-marketplace fetch**. Assigning the final verdict and editing the ledger are the controller's, not this item's.

**Dates.** Every retrieval date in this decision is **UTC**. Revision 1's checks ran 2026-09-10 05:15–05:19Z; revision 2's five read-only re-runs were appended at 05:47:55Z and 05:51:18Z (all three host logs now carry mtime 05:55:07Z, later than either recorded append time; their content is unchanged against the committed tree, so the mtime moved without the bytes moving, the cause was not investigated, and revision 2's recorded times stand). Revision 3's checks ran 2026-09-10 06:27:19Z–06:29:17Z; each new log carries its own capture timestamp in its header, and the `actionlint.log` revision-3 addendum is stamped 06:29:17Z. Revision 4's checks were captured 2026-09-10 07:08:10Z–07:22:16Z. `action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log` carries `# captured: 2026-09-10T07:08:10Z` from its own `date -u` at the head of the file and has mtime 07:22:16Z, because the log was written in five appends, the last being the E9 correction block stamped 07:22:16Z by its own `date -u`; the two revision-4 fixtures carry mtimes 07:10:05Z and 07:10:27Z, and the `actionlint.log` revision-4 addendum is stamped 07:10:37Z by its own `date -u`, matching that file's mtime. Every timestamp is read from the file's mtime or from the command's own `date -u` output; none is estimated. Every timestamp is taken from the file's own mtime or from the command's own `date -u` output at capture, never estimated. The raw reports were retrieved 2026-09-06 (`codex`) and 2026-09-09 (`opus`, all three Doxa engines).

### Principles and implementation

**Shared requirement, source, agreement level.** The requirement behind `py`'s two files is: *every pull request receives a first-pass automated review before a human reads it, and a maintainer can summon an agent into an issue or review thread without leaving GitHub.* Its source is the `py` implementation at pinned commit `b08bccfb55d05f15e46a83b52c5660b1881d19f5` (both files read verbatim, `source-repos-pinned.log`) plus ledger rows F051/F052.

The agreement level is **policy or mechanism**, the third row of the agreement-level table in the design spec §2 (`docs/superpowers/specs/2026-09-01-rs-port-research-program-design.md`), which defines it as "Any deliberately shared value, protocol detail or tool, with its owner decision or evidence", varying in "Only the aspects outside that agreement; low-level choices can be shared when that level is intentional". The spec presents the three levels **unordered**, and no ranking among them is claimed here. Revision 2 called this level "the weakest of the three levels in `docs/port/README.md`"; that was wrong twice over and is withdrawn — `docs/port/README.md` mentions "agreement level" exactly once, at line 56, in a passing clause about A5 adjudication, and carries no list of the levels and no ranking; and spec §2's definition allows the *narrowest* variation of the three, not the widest.

The assignment is argued from what §2 says rather than from a rank. §2 requires that a policy-or-mechanism agreement carry "its owner decision or evidence". There is no owner decision registering these two workflows as a shared value, and the evidence in `## Context` shows the sources already disagree. Three observations place the requirement at this level rather than at "capability or standard" or "architectural pattern":

1. `ts-launch-blueprint` at `cb1cbcb2e88b898e8c081b0abbfabc1630079c00` ships eight workflow files, none AI-related, and zero paths under `.github/` matching `claude` (`source-repos-pinned.log`: the `ls-tree` listing the eight files exits **0**; the `ls-tree … | grep -ci claude` that returns the zero count exits **1**, which for `grep -c` *is* the zero-match result — see check 6). A capability-level or pattern-level agreement would already be violated by one of the three siblings; a policy-or-mechanism agreement without a registered owner decision is exactly what an unmatched sibling looks like.
2. Nothing in the capability is ecosystem-shaped, so there is no architectural pattern whose fitness in Rust could be the thing that must agree. The action reads a diff and posts comments; the diff being Rust changes no mechanism. There is no Rust analogue to select and no Rust idiom to honor.
3. The reviewing entity is a paid third-party service reached with an organization credential. Whether to spend it here needs "its owner decision or evidence" in §2's words, which is precisely why "`py` does it" cannot settle it.

**What must agree; what may vary.** What must agree across `py`, `ts` and `rs` is the **guard contract**, because it is simultaneously a security property and a correctness property and is ecosystem-neutral. It has four clauses, and clause (a) is scoped at revision 3 to the lane it actually binds:

- (a) **The review lane** — the only lane triggered by a `pull_request` event — never runs on a pull request from a fork, on the ground that a fork run cannot obtain the credential and could only fail. That is **a platform runtime behaviour that no check here executed**; the support available is the maintainer's own `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` at `v1.0.220`, which carries the identical guard with the comment "Skip on fork PRs since they can't mint the OIDC token used for Claude API auth" (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`), plus `py`'s own guard comment recording that the action "aborts on non-human actors … which showed as a **false red check** on the v2.4.1 release PR (#470)" and that "Skipping is a neutral (non-failing) outcome for this non-required job" (`source-repos-pinned.log`) — reference concordance and one field report, not an observed run (see **U3**). Revision 2 stated clause (a) as a property of the whole contract; that was inaccurate against this decision's own fixtures, and revision 3 corrects it. **The assistant lane cannot carry this clause and does not need it**: none of its four events is a `pull_request` event, so `GITHUB_REF` is the default branch, no fork ref is checked out, and the run happens in the base repository with its credentials by design. The assistant lane's fork-adjacent exposure is a *comment* on a fork pull request, which is `#1068`'s case and is answered by clause (d), not by a same-repository test.
- (b) Neither lane runs when either the pull-request author or the run's initiator is a bot.
- (c) No credential is reachable from a run whose content an untrusted contributor controls.
- (d) **New at revision 3.** On a public repository the assistant lane never *starts* for an author outside `OWNER`, `MEMBER` or `COLLABORATOR`. This is a prefilter, not a permission check: `author_association` is not the same predicate as write access, and an organization `MEMBER` without write access passes it and is then refused by the action's own `checkWritePermissions` — which, **corrected at revision 4**, is called not by either mode but once in the shared entrypoint, `src/entrypoints/prepare.ts:44`, after `setupGitHubToken()` at `:37` (`action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`, E2). It admits every write-access holder — a repository collaborator reports `COLLABORATOR`, an organization member reports `MEMBER`, the owner reports `OWNER` — so it introduces no false refusal for a legitimate maintainer, and it is defence in depth in front of an authoritative action-side check, never a replacement for it.

What may vary per repository: whether the capability is enabled at all, which events are listened to, which model, and which prompt.

**Essential behaviors and observable acceptance criteria.**

| # | Essential behavior | Observable acceptance criterion (P03 proves each) |
|---|---|---|
| B1 | A qualifying pull-request event starts the review job | a human-authored PR in the configured origin repository produces a run with `conclusion: success` |
| B2 | Fork PRs, bot-authored PRs and bot-initiated runs are **skipped, not failed** | `.workflow_runs[0].conclusion == "skipped"` for each of: a fork PR, the release-please PR, a Dependabot PR |
| B3 | A run that starts actually posts its output | `gh api repos/<o>/<r>/issues/<pr>/comments --jq 'length'` ≥ 1; a green job with 0 comments reproduces `anthropics/claude-code-action#1121` |
| B4 | A repository created from the template with no variable set is inert | every PR and every `@claude` comment yields a **skipped** job, never a red check |
| B5 | Only a write-access user can summon the assistant | the same `@claude` comment from a non-write account produces no agent action |
| B6 | The assistant can read CI results | `@claude why did the clippy job fail?` on a red PR yields a reply naming the failing job |
| B7 | **A commenter outside `OWNER`/`MEMBER`/`COLLABORATOR` does not start the assistant job at all** | an account with no association posts `@claude …` on the public origin repository; assert `.workflow_runs \| length == 0` for a run created by that event, or `conclusion == "skipped"`; assert no runner minutes are billed for it |
| B8 | **A pull request opened as a draft and later marked ready produces exactly one review run** | open a draft PR, mark it ready; assert the review workflow has exactly one run for that PR number |
| B9 | **New at revision 4. The review lane runs its instructions from the template's own tree, with no run-time marketplace fetch** | assert the run log contains `No marketplaces specified, skipping marketplace setup` (`base-action/src/install-plugins.ts:230`) and no `Adding marketplace:` line; and assert the run still posts, which requires P03 to have authored `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` **and** the tool allowance the maintainer's own reference carries as `claude_args: --allowedTools "mcp__github_inline_comment__create_inline_comment"`. This decision does not specify that prompt's content; **that the replacement posts at all is unproven here and is U18** |

**Architectural alternatives, compared before libraries.** Four architectures can deliver "pull requests get an automated first-pass review"; the choice among them precedes the vendor choice.

| Architecture | Expressed as | Travels through "Use this template"? | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| A. Workflow-file agent (`claude-code-action`, `run-gemini-cli`, `codex-action`) | YAML in `.github/workflows/`, versioned and reviewed like code | **Yes** — the file is tree content | **Chosen.** The only architecture that is itself a template artifact. |
| B. Platform-configured reviewer (GitHub Copilot code review) | repository ruleset plus organization settings | No — repository *state*, not tree content | Rejected on fit: a template that ships nothing cannot transfer the capability, and the value test in the prompt is about two files. |
| C. Hosted third-party review app (PR-Agent as a service, CodeRabbit as an app) | a GitHub App installed on the repository | No | Rejected on the same fit ground, plus a second vendor relationship. |
| D. Self-hosted agent in a generic job | bespoke YAML plus a script | Yes | Rejected on maintenance cost: reimplements diff assembly, comment threading, deduplication and prompt-injection sanitization that A gets from a maintained action. |

Inside architecture A, `anthropics/claude-code-action` is selected over `The-PR-Agent/pr-agent`, `google-github-actions/run-gemini-cli` and `openai/codex-action` because it is the only candidate that (i) covers **both** members with one component, one credential and one App installation, (ii) is on a stable `v1` series with **no `v2` tag** among the 287 tag refs returned (`action-release.log`, HTTP 404 and `gh` exit 1 on `git/ref/tags/v2` — the correct result for a ref that does not exist), and (iii) has the maintainer running both shapes against its own repository in a form that can be read at a tag.

**Why the selected design preserves each principle.** Clauses (a) and (b) of the guard contract are carried over from `py` byte-for-byte on the review lane, so they are preserved by construction there, and clause (a) is now scoped to that lane for the reason given above. Clause (c) is preserved by the enable flag downstream and by clause (d) upstream. The two **additions** to `py`'s shape exist because `py`'s guard protects neither a template consumer nor a public origin repository:

*The enable flag, for the consumer.* In a repository created from the template, an internal PR satisfies `head.repo.full_name == github.repository`, so the fork guard passes and the job runs with no credential. The platform bounds the available fix: `jobs.<job_id>.if` may read only `github, needs, vars, inputs`; `secrets` is absent there and from `jobs.<job_id>.steps.if` as well, while it *is* available in `jobs.<job_id>.env` (verified, `contexts-availability.log`, and independently by `actionlint`, which rejects `if: secrets.X != ''` at the job level, `actionlint.log`). A `vars`-gated job is therefore the only single-line mechanism the context table permits at the job level, and its **intended** effect is that both files are inert in every unconfigured downstream repository while the origin repository needs one variable write. That intended effect is a design purpose, not an observed one: the **runtime** outcome — a `skipped` job rather than a failed one — is **U2**. The step-level alternative (`env: HAS_CRED: ${{ secrets.X != '' }}` read by `steps.if`) is legal under the same table; its expected drawback is that a skipped *step* would sit inside a job still reporting green, reading as a passing review that never happened — also a runtime expectation, reasoned from the same table and likewise never executed here.

*The author-trust prefilter, for the origin repository.* `author_association` arrives through the `github` context, which the same context table lists as available in `jobs.<job_id>.if`, so `#1068`'s suggested fix is expressible at the job level; `actionlint` accepts the four-clause form with exit 0 (`actionlint.log`, revision-3 addendum, fixture `candidate-claude.r3.yml`). The clause is written in the exact shape `#1068` asks for, `contains(fromJSON('["OWNER","MEMBER","COLLABORATOR"]'), github.event.<payload>.author_association)`, and `doxa-openai` independently asks for "a trusted association prefilter where practicable" (`raw/doxa-openai.normalized.md:388`). What the prefilter does **not** do is stated with it, three times over: it is not a write-access check (clause (d) above); it does not stop the run for an OWNER, MEMBER or COLLABORATOR who pastes untrusted text, which is the residual carried into the runner-up's third condition; and it would **not** have stopped the published chain's Phase 2, whose triggering comment came from a trusted author and whose body was tampered with afterwards — that vector is closed instead by the action's own `isBodySafeToUse` at `v1.0.220`, read in source (`action-guard-source-v1.0.220.log`, G4).

*What guards the origin repository, in order, and where each one lives.* This list exists because revision 2 rested the security case on one endpoint returning one row, and because only the last entry is in this decision's gift.

| Layer | Where it lives | Read at `v1.0.220`? | What it does not do |
|---|---|---|---|
| `author_association` prefilter | the workflow, clause (d) | expressible and lint-clean; **runtime effect is U12** | not a write-access check; no help against a trusted author quoting untrusted text |
| `checkHumanActor` | `src/github/validation/actor.ts`, called **per mode** at `src/modes/tag/index.ts:43` and `src/modes/agent/index.ts:30` — and, corrected at revision 4, **after** the trigger check and **after** the App token is minted | **yes**, G3; call sites re-read at revision 4 (E2) | consults `allowed_bots`, which this decision leaves `""` |
| `checkWritePermissions` | `src/github/validation/permissions.ts`, called **once in the shared entrypoint** at `src/entrypoints/prepare.ts:44` — **not** inside either mode (`grep -c` returns 0 in both mode files) — and after `setupGitHubToken()` at `:37` | **yes**, G1; topology corrected at revision 4 (E2) | **returns `true` unconditionally for any actor whose login ends in `[bot]`** (line 114); the refusal of App actors lives entirely in `checkHumanActor`, so these are two layers where the published bypass needed only one to fail |
| `setupGitHubToken` — **added at revision 4, and it is not a guard** | `src/github/token.ts:158`, called first at `src/entrypoints/prepare.ts:37` | **yes**, E3 | it mints rather than checks: it exchanges the OIDC token for a Claude GitHub App installation token (`token.ts:121`), requesting `contents`/`pull_requests`/`issues` `write` plus any `additional_permissions` (`token.ts:69-73`, `:100`), **before** either check above runs |
| `allowed_bots: ""`, `allowed_non_write_users: ""` | `action.yml` defaults | yes (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`) | `docs/security.md` warns that allowed bots "are not checked for repository permissions" — relevant only if a later revision sets them |
| `isBodySafeToUse` | `src/github/data/fetcher.ts:312` | **yes**, G4 | falls open when no trigger timestamp is available (`if (!triggerTime) return true`) |
| `show_full_output: false` | `docs/security.md:181` | yes, G5 | disables the run-summary channel only |
| `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` — **available at the tag but INACTIVE in this configuration** (empirical finding F2, corrected at revision 4) | `action.yml:301` emits it only as `${{ env.CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB \|\| (inputs.allowed_non_write_users != '' && '1') \|\| '' }}`; the `bubblewrap`/`socat` install at `action.yml:228` is gated `inputs.allowed_non_write_users != '' && runner.os == 'Linux'` | yes, read at the tag (E4) — but **it does not fire here** | this decision leaves `allowed_non_write_users` at `""` and neither fixture sets the `env` variable, so **the flag is empty and the isolation dependencies are not installed**. It cannot be counted as an effective control in this configuration. Even where it does fire, `docs/security.md:16` calls it "best-effort" and says it "reduces but does not eliminate prompt injection risk" |

**Reference implementations, read at a pinned tag.** All at `anthropics/claude-code-action` `v1.0.220`, retrieved 2026-09-10 (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`, `action-guard-source-v1.0.220.log`): the maintainer's own `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` (`types: [opened]` only, `contents: read` / `pull-requests: write` / `id-token: write`, federation via `vars.*`, `actions/checkout@v6`); the maintainer's own `.github/workflows/claude.yml` (four events, four-clause `@claude` condition, `contents: write` / `pull-requests: write` / `issues: write` / `id-token: write`, federation via `vars.*`, and `uses: …@main` — dogfooding, **not** a pattern to copy into a template); `examples/pr-review-comprehensive.yml` (`pull-requests: write`); `examples/claude.yml` (whose `on:` and `if:` blocks are **character-identical** to `py`'s `claude.yml`); `examples/claude-wif.yml`. **None of these five carries an `author_association` prefilter**, which is what `#1068` is about; reference concordance therefore corroborates the trigger list and the permission set and says nothing in favour of the missing gate, and is not treated as if it did. The `code-review@claude-code-plugins` plugin `py` invokes exists (`anthropics/claude-code` `contents/plugins/code-review` returns `.claude-plugin`, `README.md`, `commands`), and **revision 4 removes it from the recommendation** — see "The unpinned marketplace fetch, adjudicated" above, where the trust edge `opus` records (`raw/opus.md:589`) is decided rather than handed on. The maintainer's own review reference is now doubly load-bearing: it not only corroborates the trigger list and the permission set but supplies the pinned prompt form that replaces the marketplace fetch — `prompt: "/review-pr REPO: … PR_NUMBER: …"` at `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml:32`, resolving to `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` in the action's own tree (E7). Neither that workflow nor `examples/pr-review-comprehensive.yml` passes `plugin_marketplaces` or `plugins` at all (`grep -c` returns 0 for both).

**Maturity, dependability and integration cost.** `anthropics/claude-code-action`: `stargazers_count` 8833, `archived: false`, `pushed_at` 2026-09-09T20:12:20Z, `license.spdx_id: MIT`, created 2025-05-19 (`action-release.log`). Newest non-prerelease `v1.0.220`, published 2026-09-09T20:12:09Z; `v1.0.212`→`v1.0.220` is 8 patch releases in 8 days, so **cadence ≈ one patch per day**. Cross-ecosystem adoption: `search/code` for the action under `.github/workflows` returns `total_count: 20480`. Rust-ecosystem adoption: `org:rust-lang` returns `total_count: 0`. Maintenance state by the rubric: **active** — a release on the retrieval date makes the six-month trigger unreachable.

The negative signal is **triage, and it is now stated with the figure two engines computed rather than deferred**: 457 open issues, and of the ten most recently opened issues **zero received a first response from an `OWNER`, `MEMBER` or `COLLABORATOR`; unanswered = 10; median time to first maintainer response is not computable from a sample with no responses**. `opus` computed this over `#1818, #1817, #1798, #1796, #1795, #1788, #1781, #1779, #1777, #1776` (created 2026-09-02 to 2026-09-09), noting `#1817` accumulated 12 community comments with no maintainer reply (`raw/opus.md:167`); `codex` computed the same result independently on 2026-09-06 over its own ten (`raw/codex.md:91`). Both samples were days old at retrieval, so neither supports an `at-risk` verdict on its own. The sharper datum is `#1068` itself: the maintainer's own labels mark it `p1` and `area:permissions`, and it has stood open with zero comments for **six months** (2026-03-18 → 2026-09-10) — a security-relevant, self-triaged priority-one issue that no maintainer has answered. That is the dependability figure a consumer of this action should weigh, and revision 2 recorded it as a comment count. Integration cost is one YAML file per member plus one Anthropic Console setup; there is **no per-run cost, token or latency figure** — `#1482` ("Feature request: expose per-run cost and token usage as Action outputs", open since 2026-07-08) is the evidence that the figure is not obtainable, so cost is reasoned structurally and recorded as a gap, never estimated.

**Fitness gates, answered before popularity.** No candidate fails a gate. Both members are GitHub Actions workflows, not Rust crates, so the crate-shaped figures are `inapplicable` with the reason stated rather than omitted: **90-day and all-time downloads** — inapplicable, crates.io has no record of a GitHub Action; **RustSec advisories** — inapplicable, RustSec indexes crates.io packages. (1) **License** — pass: `MIT`, compatible with `MIT OR Apache-2.0`, and invoking an action propagates no license term into the template's artifacts. (2) **MSRV** — inapplicable: a composite action (`runs.using: "composite"`) implemented in TypeScript on Bun; it never enters `Cargo.toml` or the dependency tree, so `msrv-policy` is not engaged.

**(3) Advisory and `unsafe` posture — rewritten at revision 3, because revision 2's version of this gate was wrong about the security history.** The RustSec half is inapplicable. The GitHub advisory database analogue is applicable, was checked, and is **partial**: `repos/anthropics/claude-code-action/security-advisories` returns exactly one published advisory, and revision 2 presented that single row as the security history. It is not. The record has at least two distinct entries:

| # | What | Severity | Disclosed | Fixed | GitHub advisory? | Exploited in the wild? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| S1 | `GHSA-8q5r-mmjf-575q` / `CVE-2026-47751` — malicious `.mcp.json` in an attacker-controlled PR head plus `enableAllProjectMcpServers` → arbitrary code execution on the runner and exfiltration of workflow secrets | `medium` | published 2026-05-20 | `1.0.74` | **yes** | not stated |
| S2 | A permission-bypass plus indirect-prompt-injection chain disclosed by RyotaK of GMO Flatt Security: a GitHub App installation token bypasses `checkWritePermissions`, an issue-triage workflow with `allowed_non_write_users: "*"` yields an `issues: write` token, and that token tampers with a trusted user's issue body so a **tag-mode** run exfiltrates `ACTIONS_ID_TOKEN_REQUEST_TOKEN` / `_URL` and exchanges them for a Claude GitHub App installation token with full write access | Anthropic "rated these vulnerabilities **7.8 under CVSS v4.0**" and paid $3,800 + a $1,000 bypass bonus | published 2026-06-01; reported 2026-01-12 and 2026-01-17 | "fixed them as of Claude Code GitHub Actions **v1.0.94**" | **no** | **yes** — "Variants of the misconfiguration issues described in this article were actively exploited in the wild before this article was published"; the timeline records a similar misconfiguration in Cline's GitHub Actions exploited in the wild on **2026-02-17** |

S2's source is <https://flatt.tech/research/posts/poisoning-claude-code-one-github-issue-to-break-the-supply-chain/>, HTTP 200, retrieved 2026-09-10 UTC (`flatt-disclosure-2026-06-01.log`). **`doxa-gemini` cited it and revision 2 dismissed it on a category error.** Revision 2 wrote that gemini's "patched definitively in `v1.0.94`" claim "does not match the record" because the one published advisory was patched in `1.0.74` and because `v1.0.94`'s release notes describe a `PATH` change for `allowed_non_write_users`. Both comparisons were against the wrong object: gemini's claim is about **S2**, not S1, and "fixed as of v1.0.94" is a statement about the cumulative fixed state, not about one release's changelog. Read against its own primary source, **gemini's version figure is corroborated, not refuted**, and gemini's characterisation — "sophisticated bot accounts could successfully spoof authorization mechanisms and execute arbitrary prompt injections to steal OpenID Connect tokens" (`raw/doxa-gemini.normalized.md:185`) — is an accurate summary of S2. What stays rejected is gemini's **pin**: `v1.0.175` is `py`'s pin and 45 patch releases behind current, and currency, not safety, is the ground (see `## Engines` §3).

*Is the recommended configuration exposed to S2?* Phase by phase, and the answer is no as published, with the residual named:

- **Phase 1** needs a second workflow with `allowed_non_write_users: "*"` and `issues: write` — `examples/issue-triage.yml`. This decision recommends no such workflow and leaves `allowed_non_write_users` at its `""` default (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`). **Not present.**
- **Phase 2** needs (i) a tag-mode workflow with `id-token: write` — the recommended assistant lane *is* exactly that shape, and the article's reproduction of "the default workflow created when you set up Claude Code GitHub Actions" has an `on:` block and a four-clause `@claude` `if:` character-identical to `py`'s and to the revision-2 fixture; (ii) an `issues: write` token obtained in Phase 1, unavailable without it; and (iii) the ability to edit the triggering body after the trigger, which `isBodySafeToUse` closes at `v1.0.220` (G4). **The shape is present; the chain is not.**
- **The App-trigger bypass** that Phase 1 originally relied on is closed by `checkHumanActor`, which both modes call at `v1.0.220` (G3) — with the residual, read in source and recorded above, that `checkWritePermissions` alone still returns `true` for any `[bot]`-suffixed actor.
- **The recommended pin is above both fix versions**: `v1.0.220` > `1.0.94` > `1.0.74`. `py`'s `v1.0.175` is also above both, so the earlier reading that `py` is safe on this axis stands.
- **What is not closed** is the class. `docs/security.md` at `v1.0.220` describes prompt-injection sanitization and says plainly that "new bypass techniques may emerge", and revision 3 records the provenance the auditor asked for: several defaults this decision reads as design features are **incident remediations from S2** — the App-trigger refusal, the disabled run-summary channel, the subprocess environment scrub, and the edited-body rule. Knowing that, `docs/security.md`'s assurances are weighed as a maintainer's account of fixes already forced, not as evidence that the surface was designed safe.
- **Corrected at revision 4 (empirical finding F2): three of those four remediations are active in the recommended configuration; the subprocess environment scrub is not.** Revision 3 wrote that the four were "present at the pin" and used the set as evidence for the recommendation. Present at the pin and active in this configuration are different claims. `action.yml:301` emits `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` as `${{ env.CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB || (inputs.allowed_non_write_users != '' && '1') || '' }}`, and the step that installs its isolation dependencies, `action.yml:228`, is gated `inputs.allowed_non_write_users != '' && runner.os == 'Linux'`. This decision leaves `allowed_non_write_users` at its `""` default and neither fixture sets the `env` variable, so the flag evaluates to the empty string and `bubblewrap`/`socat` are never installed (`action-entrypoint-plugins-v1.0.220.log`, E4). **The scrub is therefore recorded as available but inactive, and is not counted as a control here.** This does not restore S2 Phase 1 — that needs `allowed_non_write_users: "*"`, which is the same default keeping the scrub off. **The decision declines to activate it**, because the only workflow-side activation path is setting `env: CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB: '1'`, which satisfies `:301` but **not** `:228`, producing the flag without the isolation dependencies it names; the effect of that half-state is not readable from the source and is recorded as **U16** for P03. Revision 3 held the fact that refutes its own claim: fitness gate 4 already recorded that all three `runner.os` guards are unreached because `allowed_non_write_users` is `""`, and `action.yml:228` is gated on exactly that expression.

The reporting gap is the finding to carry forward: **S2 has no GitHub advisory**, so `repos/<o>/<r>/security-advisories` cannot be treated as the security history of this action, and any future re-verification must include a search of independent security research, not only that endpoint. That is written into the `re-verify:` line.

No `unsafe` exists (no Rust code). (4) **`ubuntu-latest, macos-latest`** — inapplicable *as a matrix gate*, with the fact stated: each member is a single non-matrix Linux job in `py`, in both examples and in both maintainer workflows; adding a macOS runner would double the spend for no additional signal, because the reviewed artifact is a diff, not a build. Windows, noted not required: partial, and the figure is exact rather than asserted — `action.yml` at `v1.0.220` carries **three** `runner.os` guards, two `!= 'Windows'` and one `== 'Linux'`, and all three are additionally gated on `inputs.allowed_non_write_users != ''`, which this decision leaves at its default `""`, so **none of the three is reached by the recommended configuration** (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`, revision-2 addendum, HTTP 200 / exit 0). (5) **Default features / async-runtime coupling** — inapplicable (no Cargo features, no Rust runtime). The action defaults that *do* matter, read from `action.yml` at `v1.0.220`: `allowed_bots: ""` ("Empty string (default) allows no bots"), `allowed_non_write_users: ""`, `trigger_phrase: "@claude"`, `label_trigger: "claude"`, `assignee_trigger` unset, `branch_prefix: "claude/"`, `use_commit_signing: "false"`, `bot_name: "claude[bot]"`, `additional_permissions: ""`. Note `label_trigger` defaults to `"claude"` — but that default is **inert under the recommended `on:` blocks**, which list no `issues: [labeled]` type, so applying a `claude` label starts no workflow run at all. It becomes a trigger path a repository did not configure only if a later revision adds `labeled` to the assistant's event list, which is the reason to record it here. (6) **Binary size / compile time** — inapplicable; the analogue: no `cargo` invocation, therefore zero added compile time; fixed overhead is a checkout plus a Bun install; variable cost is one agent session per triggering event, unmeasurable per `#1482`.

**Baseline findings.** Every `BASELINE-REVIEW:` line any engine raised is carried here in the required `BASELINE-REVIEW: F### — principle — proposed change — evidence` form (`research/RUNBOOK.md` §P02-T01). Revision 2 carried two and stated that `opus` was the only engine to raise any; both statements were false, and revision 3 withdraws them. A controller check with an anchored `grep -c '^BASELINE-REVIEW:'` finds **six lines across four of the five reports** (`codex` 1, `opus` 2, `doxa-openai` 2, `doxa-perplexity` 1, `doxa-gemini` 0); `doxa-gemini` raised a **seventh** at `raw/doxa-gemini.normalized.md:25` wrapped in backticks, which an anchored grep cannot see. **Seven were raised in substance; all seven are carried below.** `research/RUNBOOK.md` §4 makes the controller — not this item — the adjudicator of these findings "with the affected decision owners", reconciling ledger, index, prompt and dependency changes together. R18's position on each is recorded so the controller has it; **routing is not conditional on R18 agreeing**, and the two R18 rejects are routed exactly like the two it adopts.

BASELINE-REVIEW: F051 — a least-privilege grant must never silently disable the workflow's only output — raise the review job's `pull-requests: read` to `pull-requests: write` — evidence: `py .github/workflows/claude-code-review.yml` at pinned commit `b08bccfb` grants `contents: read` / `pull-requests: read` / `issues: read` / `id-token: write` while instructing the `code-review` plugin to post with `--comment` (read verbatim, `source-repos-pinned.log`, exit 0), whereas all three maintainer references grant `pull-requests: write` — `anthropics/claude-code-action` `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` and `examples/pr-review-comprehensive.yml` at `v1.0.220`, and `docs/setup.md`'s federation sample (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`, HTTP 200) — and open issue `anthropics/claude-code-action#1121` ("claude-code-review workflow template generates insufficient permissions; action silently swallows write failure", opened 2026-03-24, still `open` at 2026-09-10) states "Posting review comments requires `pull-requests: write`" and reports the `read` variant producing a green job that posts nothing, independently reproduced in-thread on 2026-05-09 by an account whose `author_association` is `NONE`, i.e. an unrelated third party (`action-issues.log`; <https://github.com/anthropics/claude-code-action/issues/1121>, <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anthropics/claude-code-action/v1.0.220/.github/workflows/claude-review.yml>, <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anthropics/claude-code-action/v1.0.220/examples/pr-review-comprehensive.yml>, all retrieved 2026-09-10 UTC). Raised by `opus` (`raw/opus.md:93`). Affected items: **R18** (this item) and **R08**, which owns the repository-wide permission-hardening convention this row must not be read as overriding. **R18's position: adopted**, with limits below.

BASELINE-REVIEW: F051 and F052 — minimize long-lived credentials in a public template — prefer workload identity federation over a static `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` repository secret — evidence: both `py` files at `b08bccfb` pass `claude_code_oauth_token: ${{ secrets.CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN }}` (`source-repos-pinned.log`); `docs/setup.md#workload-identity-federation` at `v1.0.220` documents exchanging the workflow's GitHub OIDC token for a short-lived Anthropic access token — "no `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` secret to create, store, or rotate" — via `anthropic_federation_rule_id`, `anthropic_organization_id` and `anthropic_service_account_id`, values the document calls "identifiers, not credentials, so they can live directly in the workflow file (or in repository variables)", and **both** maintainer workflows at `v1.0.220` use exactly this with no `secrets.` reference anywhere (`action-source-v1.0.220.log`). Federation requires `id-token: write`, which `docs/setup.md` notes "the default GitHub App authentication path already requires", so it costs no additional permission (<https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anthropics/claude-code-action/v1.0.220/docs/setup.md>, <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anthropics/claude-code-action/v1.0.220/.github/workflows/claude.yml>, retrieved 2026-09-10 UTC; the vendor's own federation reference is <https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/manage-claude/workload-identity-federation>). Raised by `opus` (`raw/opus.md:95`). Affected items: **R18** and **R21**, which owns bot-credential sourcing for the contributors bot and should not be read as constrained by this row. **R18's position: adopted**, with limits below, and with the correction that federation is **not** a mitigation for `#1068`.

BASELINE-REVIEW: F051/F052 — optional AI assistance must not impose a paid credential, privileged GitHub App, or provider run on every public-template consumer — change the baseline from enabled `py`-derived workflows to an absent-by-default, documented opt-in pair — evidence: Anthropic requires provider authentication and repository authority, while GitHub withholds ordinary secrets from fork PRs; retrieved 2026-09-06: <https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anthropics/claude-code-action/main/docs/setup.md>; <https://docs.github.com/en/actions/reference/workflows-and-actions/events-that-trigger-workflows>. Raised by `codex` (`raw/codex.md:21`). Affected items: **R18**. **R18's position: not adopted as the baseline change, and the reason is narrow** — the enable flag makes the pair absent *in effect* for every consumer (`vars` unset ⇒ no run, intended; **U2**), which the empirical checks show is expressible at the job level, so the imposition the finding objects to is answered without removing the files. The finding's underlying judgement — that this should not be on by default anywhere — is the **ranked runner-up** and is live: see the three conditions under which it wins. Routed unchanged.

BASELINE-REVIEW: F051 — credential-free and non-metered template defaults — do not port the active automatic Claude PR-review workflow; retain it only as an opt-in example and treat removal from `py` as a follow-on project — evidence: automatic runs consume provider capacity, fork and Dependabot events have restricted credentials, and the Rust practice survey found no ecosystem convention requiring it (<https://docs.github.com/en/actions/reference/workflows-and-actions/events-that-trigger-workflows>). Raised by `doxa-openai` (`raw/doxa-openai.normalized.md:138`). Affected items: **R18**, and **`py-launch-blueprint`** as a follow-on project, which is outside this programme's remit. **R18's position: not adopted**, on the same ground as the `codex` row, and noting the concrete Rust-practice figure that supports it: `org:rust-lang` returns `total_count: 0` for this action, so no Rust convention requires it in either direction. Routed unchanged.

BASELINE-REVIEW: F052 — AI ChatOps is repository-operator policy rather than a language capability — do not port an active `@claude` workflow; document it as a separately adoptable example **and split its ledger decision from F051** — evidence: it has different triggers, write permissions, cost frequency, and actor controls from automatic review (<https://github.com/smorinlabs/py-launch-blueprint/blob/main/.github/workflows/claude.yml>). Raised by `doxa-openai` (`raw/doxa-openai.normalized.md:140`). Affected items: **R18** and **the ledger** (`docs/port/COMMONALITY.md:57-58`, which bundles F051 and F052 to one item). **This row carries a ledger-shaped ask and is routed as such**; R18 cannot split a ledger row and does not attempt to. R18's position on the *design* question is recorded in `## Engines` §6 (bundle, on a stated coupling argument that weakens if `synchronize` is restored); R18's position on the *ledger* question is that it belongs to the controller. Revision 2 discussed the design question and never routed the ledger ask; revision 3 routes it.

BASELINE-REVIEW: F051 — templates should expose a safe, optional capability for AI-assisted PR review and conversational assistance via maintained actions — treat the absence of these workflows in `ts-launch-blueprint` as accidental drift rather than a deliberate policy difference, and align future TypeScript and Rust templates on shipping both workflows with ecosystem-specific trigger tuning — evidence: `anthropics/claude-code-action` supports both automatic review and `@mention` assistance from one action family, and `py`'s implementation shows the two can coexist when guarded against bot-authored PRs and repository permission checks. Raised by `doxa-perplexity` (`raw/doxa-perplexity.normalized.md:41`). Affected items: **R18**, **the ledger** (the `ts` side of F051/F052), and any future `ts` port. **R18's position: rejected with reasons, and routed anyway.** The reason: the finding asserts `ts`'s omission is *accidental*, and the agreement-level argument above reaches the opposite conclusion from the same evidence — the level is policy or mechanism under spec §2, which requires "its owner decision or evidence", and no owner decision registers this pair as a shared value, so an unmatched sibling is what a policy-level agreement without a registered decision looks like, not drift. The finding also offers no evidence *about `ts`* — no `ts` commit, issue or decision is cited — and `TS_PORT_DECISIONS.md` has no entry either way (`inputs/prompt.md`, `## Context`). A claim about another repository's intent needs evidence from that repository. Routing is unaffected: §4 gives the controller the call.

BASELINE-REVIEW: F051, F052 — Policy — adopt bundled AI workflows across templates — the TypeScript template's omission represents accidental drift or a missing capability rather than a justified architectural departure; adopting the bundled workflows in `rs-launch-blueprint` aligns with `py-launch-blueprint` and reinforces baseline AI automation across the organization's foundational templates, with the Python implementation as the definitive reference architecture. Raised by `doxa-gemini` (`raw/doxa-gemini.normalized.md:25`, written inside backticks, which is why an anchored `grep` does not see it). Affected items: **R18**, **the ledger**, and any future `ts` port. **R18's position: rejected with reasons, and routed anyway.** Two reasons, beyond the `doxa-perplexity` row's: the finding's own framing — "the Python implementation serves as the definitive reference architecture" — is the failure mode the research mandate (spec §2, owner amendment A5) exists to prevent, since source implementations are evidence and not predetermined winners; and this decision does *not* take `py` as-is, changing one permission, the credential path, one trigger list, and adding three guards. Routed unchanged.

**Adjudication of the two adopted findings — what the evidence supports and what it does not.** Both are **accepted for the `rs` template**, with limits stated because both are security-relevant.

*F051 / `pull-requests: write`.* **Supported:** that `py`'s grant differs from every maintained reference; that the reference configurations which post comments all grant `write`; that a third party reports and a second party independently reproduces a silent no-op under `read`. **Not supported:** that `py`'s own repository currently exhibits the silent failure — no run of `py`'s workflow was inspected, and `py`'s `--comment` flag differs from the reproducer's configuration, which the reproducer describes as lacking `--comment`. **Not established by this research:** the exact mechanism, i.e. whether the job's `pull-requests` grant bounds the Claude GitHub App installation token used for the write, or whether some writes go through the job's own `GITHUB_TOKEN`. `docs/security.md` says only that "The GitHub app receives only a short-lived token scoped specifically to the repository" and that "the token cannot … perform actions beyond the configured permissions", which does not disambiguate the two. The recommendation therefore rests on *concordance with three maintained references plus a reproduced report*, not on a demonstrated causal chain, and B3 above is the check that settles it in P03. Consequence if the finding is wrong: an over-broad grant on the review job, which R08 would then narrow — a strictly smaller failure than a review that silently never posts.

*F051+F052 / workload identity federation.* **Supported:** that federation exists at `v1.0.220`, that it removes the stored credential, that the maintainer uses it in production, and that it adds no permission beyond the `id-token: write` already required. **Two costs, both verified and both material:** `docs/setup.md` requires **admin access to an Anthropic organization** (register an issuer, create a service account, create a federation rule) — a one-time setup each downstream fork owner must repeat, which a `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` produced by `claude setup-token` does not require; and "Inline comment classification (`classify_inline_comments`) currently requires `anthropic_api_key`; with federation it is skipped and unconfirmed inline comments are posted directly" — a behavior change, not merely a credential change. **Corrected at revision 3:** federation is **not** a mitigation for `#1068`. It removes the static secret from the runner (`#1068`'s second exposure) and leaves the first and third — runner minutes and an obtainable OIDC token — exactly where they were, and the third is what the S2 chain exfiltrates. Revision 2 carried `opus`'s framing of federation as one of two `#1068` mitigations; only clause (d) is one. **Not established:** whether `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` (which `docs/setup.md` describes as generated by Pro and Max users with `claude setup-token`) and a federated service-account token bill against the same account, so the finding is recorded as *prefer federation where the owner has an Anthropic organization and is not relying on subscription-backed OAuth*, and the static token stays documented as the supported fallback. `docs/setup.md` also warns: "Do not set `anthropic_api_key` or `claude_code_oauth_token` alongside the federation inputs — a static credential takes precedence and federation will not be used."

**Minimal realistic example and executable acceptance check.** The minimal example is the two **revision-4** lint fixtures at `review/evidence/candidate-claude-code-review.r4.yml` and `review/evidence/candidate-claude.r4.yml`, plus two repository variables, three federation identifier variables and the template-local `.claude/commands/review-pr.md` that P03 must author. `actionlint` on both exits 0 (`actionlint.log`, revision-4 addendum); that is the whole of what can be executed before the template exists. `candidate-claude-code-review.r4.yml` differs from the revision-3 fixture by the removal of `plugin_marketplaces` and `plugins` and the substitution of the template-local prompt; `candidate-claude.r4.yml`'s YAML is **byte-identical** to the revision-3 fixture's below the header comment (`diff` exit 0, recorded in the revision-4 addendum), and its header is rewritten to correct the call topology and to state what an adopter is accepting. The revision-2 and revision-3 fixtures are left byte-for-byte as they were linted and audited, and remain in the directory as the prior state. B1–B8 above are the acceptance criteria and every one of them needs a live run, a configured GitHub App and a credential, so all eight are **proposed**, none executed, and all are represented in the unproven list under `## Engines`.

re-verify: 2026-12-10, or earlier on any of — **the owner recording acceptance or refusal of the residual named under "What enabling costs the owner", which is the condition the recommendation now turns on**; **`anthropics/claude-code-action` making `CLAUDE_CODE_SUBPROCESS_ENV_SCRUB` active independently of `allowed_non_write_users`, or making a marketplace URL pinnable to a ref (`MARKETPLACE_URL_REGEX` at `base-action/src/install-plugins.ts:7-8` ceasing to anchor on `.git`), either of which would reopen a revision-4 decision**; a `v2` tag appearing on `anthropics/claude-code-action`, or the repository becoming archived; **a new published security advisory beyond `GHSA-8q5r-mmjf-575q`, *or* a new independent disclosure with no advisory, which is why re-verification must search security research and not only `repos/<o>/<r>/security-advisories`**; **`#1068` closing, or the shipped `examples/claude.yml` gaining an author-trust gate, either of which changes whether clause (d) is a local addition or upstream default**; `#1121` closing with a change to the recommended permissions; `#1482` closing, which would make the dropped `synchronize` trigger a numeric rather than a structural decision; the maintainer's own `.github/workflows/claude-review.yml` adding `synchronize`; `docs/security.md` correcting its "no `ref:` → base branch" annotation, which is wrong for `pull_request` events; `google-github-actions/run-gemini-cli` or `openai/codex-action` publishing a `v1` release series with notes; or R08, R09, R20 or R45 publishing a decision that contradicts a configuration recorded here. The version pin is stale on a scale of days at roughly one patch release per day and must be re-resolved unconditionally at implementation time.

## Parameters

- owns — **none**. R18's `## Couplings` block has an empty `owns:` line and `research/CLAUDE.md` records R18's `owns` column as `—`, so this item contributes no row to `docs/port/PARAMETERS.md`.
- assumes msrv-policy = stable minus 2 minor versions, raised only in a minor release, declared as rust-version in Cargo.toml and tested in CI
- assumes rust-edition = 2024
- assumes target-os-matrix = ubuntu-latest, macos-latest
- assumes license = MIT OR Apache-2.0
- No `CONFLICT:` line is emitted. R18's `## Couplings` block lists an empty `consumes:`, so there is no consumed parameter whose value this recommendation could need changed, and none of the four owner-fixed values above is challenged.

**How each assumed parameter bears on this decision.** The four lines above carry the registry value and nothing else, because the packaging validator reads everything after `= ` as the value and compares it byte-for-byte with `docs/port/PARAMETERS.md`; the engagement notes that revision 4 wrote on those lines are therefore stated here instead, unchanged in substance.

- `msrv-policy` is **not engaged**: neither member of the bundle adds a crate to the dependency tree, so no minimum supported Rust version is constrained by anything R18 recommends.
- `rust-edition` is **not engaged**: neither workflow file references a Rust edition or invokes `cargo`.
- `target-os-matrix` is **engaged only in the negative**: both members are deliberately single non-matrix jobs on one Linux runner and do not join the OS matrix, because the reviewed artifact is a diff rather than a build.
- `license` is **engaged and satisfied**: the single recommended component is MIT (`license.spdx_id`, `action-release.log`), and invoking a GitHub Action propagates no license term into the template's distributed artifacts.

## Empirical check

Working directory for every command: `/Users/stevemorin/c/rs-launch-blueprint-p02-plan` (except the `actionlint` invocations and the revision-3 log captures, whose working directory is `research/runs/R18/2026-09-06T043454Z-6f31d86e5b7d/review/evidence`). Toolchain and OS, captured in `review/evidence/toolchain.log`:

```
$ rustc --version
rustc 1.98.0 (88d9e12ae 2026-08-18) (Homebrew)
$ cargo --version
cargo 1.98.0 (797e8a9bc 2026-08-05) (Homebrew)
$ uname -srm
Darwin 25.4.0 arm64          # macOS 26.4 (build 25E246)
$ actionlint --version
1.7.12
$ gh --version
gh version 2.98.0 (2026-08-20)
```

`rustc` and `cargo` are recorded because the role contract requires them; **neither member invokes them**, so they bound nothing in this decision. Every check below **verifies a claim the raw reports, the upstream issues or the audits make**; none builds the recommendation. All **seven** candidate workflow files are **lint fixtures under `review/evidence/`**, never under `.github/workflows/`, and each carries a header saying so. No workflow was installed, no credential was used, no provider run occurred, and nothing was written outside this run's `review/` directory.

**How to read the `Exit` column.** It names the **process exit of each command**, taken strictly from the cited log, and it is not a verdict on the check. Three commands exit **non-zero as their expected and correct result**, because for those tools a non-zero exit *is* the finding: `gh api` exits 1 on HTTP 404, and a 404 on `git/ref/tags/v2` is the evidence that no `v2` major series exists; `grep -c` exits 1 when it matches nothing, and a zero count on `ts-launch-blueprint`'s `.github/` tree is the evidence that `ts` ships no `claude` path; `actionlint` exits 1 on the negative fixture, which is the diagnostic being sought. Where a log records no exit for a command, the cell says so rather than inferring one.
